# Practitioner review packet — SmartCiti.X ~VR Simulators and Trade Skills Simulator

_Generated by `node tools/gen_review_packet.mjs` from the station modules; regenerate after any content change. Do not edit by hand — record verdicts in the sign-off tables and commit, or transcribe them to the tracking system._

**Purpose.** Every station here states a real union trade and a real certification or standard, and scores a learner against an ordered procedure with seeded hazards. Before any training record from a station is treated as evidence of readiness for that certification, a qualified practitioner in the trade reviews the station: is the order of operations right, is every rationale true, is every hazard's consequence realistic, and is the certification claim the right one? This packet is the review document. One section per station, with a sign-off block.

**Two signatures per section.** The first is automated and already present: a *preview* verdict from the generator confirming the section is complete and internally consistent (category, trade and certification named; a rationale on every step; a judgeable consequence on every hazard; late notes on real targets; the scene builds inside the headset mesh budget; every dossier statement sourced). The second is the practitioner's, and it is blank until a named person signs it. A preview pass is the entry ticket to review, not a stand-in for it.

**How to review.** Play the station once (`smartcity/index.html?sim=<id>` or `trades/index.html?room=<id>`), then work down its tables. Mark each step's rationale and each hazard's consequence yes or no with a note. Sign the block. A station with any "no" is not approved until the change is made and the section regenerated.

**Roster.** 89 stations and rooms across 13 categories. Automated preview passed on 89/89 on 2026-09-21. Practitioner sign-off: 0/89.

# Energy & Power

## Charge Point (SmartCiti.X · `charge-point`)

**Category:** Energy & Power  
**Trade:** EV service technician  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IBEW — NFPA 70E arc-flash qualified, EVITP-certified EV infrastructure technician  
**Tagline:** DC fast-charger fault isolation, capacitor discharge and busbar torque  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-21 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the work order and fault code | Select | The fault code tells you which module and which isolation points. Walking up and opening doors is how you isolate the wrong cabinet. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | End the charging session | Select | A controlled stop ramps the current to zero and unlocks the connector. Everything after this assumes no load on the cable. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Establish the work zone | Sequence (any order: cone at the bay entry, cone at the kerb side, barrier across the bay) | A charging bay is a live traffic lane. The work zone stops a driver reversing onto you while your head is inside a cabinet. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Don class 0 gloves and face shield | Select | Class 0 rubber gloves with leather protectors and a face shield, before the door opens. The DC link does not care that the AC supply is off. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Open the upstream AC disconnect | Turn | Isolate at the source, not at the charger's own contactor. A control-level stop is not an isolation. The handle goes fully over — a disconnect stopped halfway is a disconnect that has not opened. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Lock and tag the disconnect | Select | Your lock, your key. A charger that reboots itself while your hands are inside is a charger someone else energised. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Wait out the DC link discharge | Timed hold | The bleed resistors need their stated time. Counting to ten and opening the panel is how technicians find out what a charged DC link feels like. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Prove the DC link is dead | Graded reading | Absence of voltage is measured, not assumed. Below the manufacturer's safe threshold — anything else and you wait longer. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Withdraw the faulty power module | Carry and place | Modules are hot-swap rated in service, not in fault. With the link proven dead you can handle it without the arc risk. It goes straight onto the cart, because a module left on the ground gets stepped on and one left in the van gets fitted to the next job. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Torque the busbar connection | Graded reading | Under-torqued busbars run hot and eventually weld themselves; over-torqued ones crack the lug. The number on the label is the number. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Restore and test-charge | Select | The job is not finished at the repair. A test session under load is what proves the fix and clears the unit back to service. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `dc-busbar` | That is the DC link busbar. The supply being open means nothing here — the capacitors hold hundreds of volts for minutes after shutdown, and they will not let go once you are across them. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `damaged-cable` | That cable has a cut through to the screen. A damaged DC lead on a 350 kW charger is not a cosmetic fault; bin it and tag the unit out of service. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `interlock-bypass` | That jumper defeats the door interlock. Every bypass ever fitted 'just for testing' is still fitted. The interlock is the last thing standing between the next technician and the DC link. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `live-connector` | The vehicle is still in a charging session. Pulling a CCS connector under load draws a DC arc that will not self-extinguish — stop the session at the vehicle or the charger first. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Solar Deck (SmartCiti.X · `solar-deck`)

**Category:** Energy & Power  
**Trade:** Solar / BESS technician  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IBEW — NABCEP PV Installation Professional certified  
**Tagline:** Rooftop array and battery commissioning: fall protection, rapid shutdown and string test  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-21 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Survey the roof and anchor plan | Select | Falls from height are the single largest killer in this trade, which is exactly why OSHA's fall-protection rules exist. You establish where the certified anchors are and exactly where the unprotected edges start before you ever step onto the deck, not while you are already standing three paces from one. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Connect to the anchor | Select | The anchor is rated to 22 kN and inspected on a schedule the way ANSI's fall-protection standards require. Connecting to a vent stack or a conduit run instead because it happens to be closer is exactly how anchors fail at the one moment their rating actually gets tested. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Don and check the harness | Select | The harness is inspected at every single use, not on a calendar schedule. A lanyard with a fraying strand or a D-ring with a hairline crack passes a casual glance right up until the one fall it is asked to actually arrest. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Initiate rapid shutdown | Select | Rapid shutdown drops the array conductors outside the modules to a safe touch voltage within seconds of the initiator being pressed. It is the rapid-shutdown requirement that makes rooftop PV wiring approachable in daylight at all — without it, an array under sun is simply a live circuit. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Open the DC disconnect | Turn | DC first, always. Opening the AC side while DC is still feeding the inverter leaves stored energy and a live front end sitting behind the cover you are about to work near — the inverter has to lose its source before it loses its grid connection. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Open the AC disconnect | Turn | Now the inverter has neither a DC source feeding it nor a grid connection to push back into. Both sides open, in that order, is what makes the equipment actually isolated for electrical safe work purposes under NFPA 70E, rather than just sitting quiet. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Lock both disconnects | Select | Two isolations, two locks, because either one alone leaves a path back to a live conductor. An IBEW crew treats an inverter that gets reset remotely mid-string-test as a near miss, not an inconvenience — the locks are what stop it from happening at all. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Measure string open-circuit voltage | Graded reading | Measuring Voc against the expected value for this string's module count, wiring and temperature is how you find a missing module, a reversed pair or a shaded run before you ever energise — after commissioning, the same fault just looks like reduced output nobody can explain. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Complete the string tests in order | Sequence (strict order: polarity, insulation resistance, earth continuity) | Polarity first, because an insulation test on a reversed string tells you nothing useful and can damage the tester by driving current the wrong way through it. Earth continuity comes last because it confirms the protective bonding path every reading before it has been quietly relying on. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Check the battery state | Graded reading | A battery commissioned at the extremes of its charge window — nearly empty or nearly full — cannot be balanced properly across its cells, and the first charge cycle is exactly when that imbalance shows up as one cell ageing faster than the other five. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Energise and record | Select | Energise AC then DC — the exact reverse of how you isolated — watch the inverter come up clean, and record every reading you took. The commissioning record is what the next technician inherits instead of guessing at this array's baseline from scratch. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `roof-edge` | You walked to an unprotected edge with no anchor. Falls from height are the single largest killer in this trade and the roof does not care how experienced you are. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `hot-mc4` | You pulled an MC4 under load. A live DC string will not stop arcing when the plug separates — the array is a current source for as long as the sun is on it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `battery-terminal` | Those are the battery terminals. A DC battery bank has no zero-crossing: a spanner across them vaporises and takes your hand with it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `damaged-conduit` | That conduit is crushed and the conductors inside are chafed. Energising a damaged run puts fault current into the roof structure. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Line Truck (SmartCiti.X · `line-truck`)

**Category:** Energy & Power  
**Trade:** Outside / overhead lineworker (IBEW)  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IBEW — OSHA 29 CFR 1910.269 qualified electrical worker  
**Tagline:** Bucket-truck line work: rubber goods testing, isolation and correct grounding order  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-21 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Hold the tailboard briefing | Select | The minimum approach distance changes with voltage class, and both OSHA 1910.269 and the NESC set it as a table lookup by kV, not a judgment call made at the pole. Everyone on the crew hears the same number, from the same table, before anyone leaves the ground. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Set the outriggers | Select | A boom worked from an unstabilized truck can shift the whole vehicle under a lateral load of only a few hundred pounds at full extension — the outriggers are what turn a fifteen-ton truck into a fixed base rather than a pivot point. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Bond and ground the truck | Select | If the boom ever contacts an energized conductor, the bond is what keeps the truck body from becoming the fastest path to ground through a person standing on the frame or the step — without it, fault current looks for any path it can find. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Air-test the rubber gloves | Graded reading | A pinhole in rubber goods is invisible to the eye until it is under pressure. The air test finds a leak that a puncture this small would otherwise let through at full line voltage the first time the glove is actually loaded. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Inspect the rubber sleeves | Select | Sleeves cover the reach between the glove cuff and the shoulder — a cut, an ozone crack or embedded debris there is just as live a path to a phase conductor as the same defect would be in the glove itself. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Inspect the bucket liner | Select | The fibreglass liner is the insulating barrier between the worker and the boom's own conductive steel structure. A cracked or contaminated liner stops being an insulator at exactly the moment the boom needs it to be one. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Dielectric-test the boom | Graded reading | The boom's fibreglass insulation degrades with UV exposure and contamination and is tested on its own megohm schedule, not assumed sound because it looked fine yesterday or held up on the last job. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Confirm the minimum approach distance | Select | The boundary is set and marked before the boom starts moving, using the same voltage-class table OSHA 1910.269 and the NESC both publish — not judged by eye once the bucket is already close to a phase conductor. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Open the line switch | Turn | This is the isolation, and everything from here on is checked against the assumption that a switch position can still be wrong — a stuck contact or a mis-wired indicator reads open long before it actually is. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Test the line dead with a hot stick | Graded reading | The switch tells you what should be true. The hot stick tester tells you what is actually true on this specific conductor, right now, which is the only reading OSHA 1910.269 accepts before a ground goes on. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Apply personal protective grounds in order | Sequence (strict order: ground electrode clamp, neutral clamp, phase conductor clamp) | Clamping the ground end first means that if the phase turns out to be live when you make that last connection, the low-resistance path is already built and waiting for fault current instead of routing it through you. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Teach the line-crawler waypoints | Sequence (strict order: Start waypoint, Mid waypoint, End waypoint) | The crawler drives this span exactly in the order the waypoints were recorded — teach start to end, matching the direction it will actually travel along the now de-energized and grounded conductor. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 13 | Save the crawler path | Select | An untaught point list is just three recorded positions sitting in memory. Saving it is what turns those waypoints into a path the crawler controller can actually queue and run over live grounds. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 14 | Dry-run the line-crawler path | Timed hold | A brand-new path is verified at walking pace with a hand on the controller, watching the whole span, before the crawler ever runs it unattended over grounds a crew just applied minutes earlier. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 15 | Confirm rescue readiness | Select | Rescue equipment staged and checked before work starts is the only version that is actually usable in the first thirty seconds of an emergency — a hook still in its case on the truck bed helps nobody up the pole. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `energized-conductor` | You brought the bucket inside the minimum approach distance without rubber gloves and sleeves donned. Inside that distance the air gap itself is no longer your protection — the rubber is, and it goes on before the boom does. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `damaged-glove` | That rubber glove has a puncture that the air test would have caught. Wearing a failed glove is worse than wearing none, because it tells you it's protecting you right up until it doesn't. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `ground-point-untested` | You reached for the equipment ground before the tester confirmed the line was actually dead. If that conductor is still live, the ground set becomes the path to ground — through whoever is holding it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `boom-control` | That is the boom lift control. Operating the boom before the truck chassis is bonded and grounded leaves the whole vehicle, and anyone touching it, at line potential the instant the boom contacts an energized conductor. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Substation Switching (SmartCiti.X · `substation-switching`)

**Category:** Energy & Power  
**Trade:** Substation electrician / switching operator  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IBEW — utility switching and tagging authorisation; OSHA 29 CFR 1910.269 (electric power generation, transmission and distribution); NFPA 70E arc-flash PPE for the verification  
**Tagline:** Feeder outage on a written switching order: read-back, breaker open, disconnects open, test dead, grounds on, tag, hand-off  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-21 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the switching order | Select | A switching order is written by one person and checked by another before it is issued. The operator reads all of it before the first step, because a step makes sense only in the sequence it sits in. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Read back step 1 to the control centre | Select | Two people agree on every switch before it moves. The read-back is the check that the order in your hand is the order the control centre is working from. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Arc-rated PPE for the cubicle | Sequence (any order: arc-rated suit, arc-rated hood and gloves) | The cubicle label gives the incident energy. The PPE matches it, and it is on before anything in the cubicle moves. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Open the feeder breaker | Turn | The breaker is the device rated to interrupt load. It opens first so that everything downstream of it is de-energised before any disconnect moves. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Open the disconnects | Sequence (strict order: line-side disconnect, bus-side disconnect) | With the breaker open there is no current, and the disconnects can move. Two visible breaks isolate the breaker from both sources so it cannot be back-fed from either. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Test the bus dead — every phase | Graded reading | Open disconnects are a claim; the tester is the proof. Every phase, because one back-fed phase is enough. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Apply the grounds | Sequence (strict order: A-phase ground, B-phase ground, C-phase ground) | Grounds make the isolated bus safe to touch: if anything back-feeds, the fault goes to ground, not through the crew. Ground end first so the clamp is never live in your hand. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Torque the ground clamps | Timed hold | A loose ground is a ground that lifts off under fault current, exactly when it is needed. It is torqued, not tightened by feel. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Tag and lock the isolation | Select | The tag names the order, the operator and the crew that now owns the feeder. Nothing on this tag moves until that crew releases it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Walk the cubicle before the hand-off | Find (any order: cracked bus insulator) | The feeder is dead for a window. What is found now gets fixed in the window; what is missed waits for the next outage — or causes it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Report the feeder clear | Select | The hand-off closes the order. The control centre marks the feeder out of service and the maintenance crew holds it until they call it back. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `disconnect-under-load` | You went to open the line disconnect with the breaker still closed. A disconnect is not rated to break load — opening it under 12 kV of feeder current draws an arc across the blades that does not stop until something melts. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `ground-untested` | You reached for the ground clamps before the bus was tested dead. Grounds on an energised bus are a bolted fault with your hands on the lead; the test comes first, every phase, every time. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `skip-readback` | You did the step without reading it back to the control centre. The order exists so two people agree on every switch before it moves — a step done alone is a step the system operator does not know happened. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `no-arc-ppe` | You approached the cubicle for the test without arc-rated PPE. The verification is the one step where the bus might still be live; the label on the cubicle says what to wear, and it is worn. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Battery Yard (SmartCiti.X · `battery-yard`)

**Category:** Energy & Power  
**Trade:** Battery energy storage technician — grid scale  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IBEW outside construction and utility locals; NFPA 855 stationary energy storage installation; NFPA 70E DC arc-flash boundary and shock approach; OSHA 29 CFR 1910.147 lockout/tagout and 1910.269 for the utility interconnection; UL 9540A thermal-runaway test data  
**Tagline:** Grid battery module swap: gas and vent check from outside, stop from the controller, AC before DC, rack disconnects in order, bleed-down wait, live-dead-live on the DC bus, racks grounded, insulated tools, thermal check before the door  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-21 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the work order and one-line | Select | A battery yard is a dozen identical enclosures. The one-line says which one is being worked, what the DC bus does when it is opened, and what the technician must be wearing to stand in front of it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Read the enclosure from outside | Sequence (any order: gas detection panel, deflagration vents) | Everything you need to know about whether the enclosure is safe to open is readable from outside it. That is why the panel is mounted outside. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Stop the unit from the controller | Select | A controlled stop ramps the inverter down and opens the contactors in the order the system was designed for. Everything after this is opening something that is already at zero. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Open and lock the AC side | Sequence (strict order: AC breaker open, AC lock and tag) | The AC side is opened first because it is the side that can re-energise from the grid. With it locked, the only energy left in the enclosure is what the cells are holding. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Open the rack DC disconnects | Sequence (strict order: rack 1 disconnect, rack 2 disconnect, rack 3 disconnect) | Each rack is its own string at full bus voltage. They are opened one at a time, in order, so the aisle is never guessing which string is still tied in. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Wait out the bleed-down | Timed hold | The DC link capacitors hold a lethal charge after every disconnect is open. The wait is printed on the door because it is not a judgement call and it cannot be shortened by looking at the meter. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Live, dead, live on the DC bus | Sequence (strict order: meter on the known source, meter on the DC bus, meter on the known source again) | A meter that failed between the first reading and the bus reading will show any bus as dead. Proving it before and after is the only thing that makes 'dead' mean anything. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Confirm the bus is at zero | Graded reading | Zero on this bus means single digits, not 'lower than it was'. Anything above the band means a string is still tied in somewhere and the aisle is still live. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Ground the rack and take insulated tools | Sequence (any order: rack grounding cable, insulated tool set) | The ground holds the rack at zero if a string comes back. The insulated tools are what stop a dropped spanner from becoming a bus bar across two terminals. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Draw the failed module out | Carry and place | A module is sixty kilograms of cells on a rail. It comes out onto a cart, not into someone's arms, and it goes to the quarantine area, not back in the aisle. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Thermal-check the rack | Graded reading | A module fails because something got hot, and the neighbours are the first place that shows. The scan before the door closes is what catches a second bad cell before it becomes the next event. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Walk the enclosure before you close it | Find (any order: suppression bottle out of date) | The enclosure protects itself when nobody is there. Everything that does that is checked before the door closes, because the next person here may be arriving to an alarm. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `open-door-on-alarm` | You opened the enclosure with the gas detector in alarm. A lithium enclosure in off-gas is pre-ignition: opening the door gives the flammable mixture the oxygen it was missing, and the deflagration goes out the door you are standing in. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `dc-before-ac` | You opened the DC rack disconnects with the inverter still running. Breaking DC under load draws an arc that does not self-extinguish the way AC does; the disconnect welds, the arc keeps burning, and the arc-flash boundary is the whole aisle. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `metal-jewellery` | You reached into the rack wearing a ring and a watch. A battery string is a low-voltage, very-high-current source: metal across the terminals does not shock you, it welds to your hand and burns through it in seconds. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `water-on-cells` | You reached for a water extinguisher at a cell fire. Water on a burning lithium module flashes and spreads the event; the enclosure's own suppression and a safe distance are the response, and the fire service is called for the rest. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Gas Leak Survey (SmartCiti.X · `gas-leak-survey`)

**Category:** Energy & Power  
**Trade:** Gas distribution serviceperson / leak survey technician  
**Certification / standard claimed:** UWUA and USW gas-utility locals — operator-qualified for leakage survey and leak investigation; 49 CFR Part 192 (PHMSA minimum safety standards for gas distribution pipelines, including its leakage-survey, investigation and repair requirements) and the operator's own written leak-grading and re-check procedure made under it; NFPA 54 for the customer fuel-gas piping downstream of the meter; OSHA 29 CFR 1910.146 where migrated gas puts a manhole or a vault into the survey  
**Tagline:** Reported odour over a distribution main: instrument zeroed in clean air, ignition sources controlled, bar-holed outward along the trench and the utilities, graded on where the gas is, inside readings evacuated rather than investigated  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-21 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Take the odour report | Select | An odour call is somebody else's observation, and it is the only evidence there is until the instrument comes out of the truck. Whether it was smelled outdoors at the kerb or indoors in a cellar changes the approach before you leave the vehicle, and the time it was reported tells you whether you are arriving at a leak that has been filling a void under the pavement since last night or one that started while the caller was still on the phone. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Park upwind, shut down, and walk the last part | Sequence (strict order: truck parked upwind and short of the call, engine and heater shut down, intrinsically safe kit only, carried in) | The first vehicle to arrive at a gas call parks where the gas is not, because a running engine sitting over a venting trench is the ignition source most likely to be present and the only one entirely within your control. Shutting it down also stops the cab heater and the beacon inverter. What goes in from there is the instrument, the bar and a torch rated for a flammable atmosphere; the phone and the ordinary lamp stay in the cab with the truck. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Zero the combustible gas indicator in clean air | Timed hold | A catalytic combustible gas indicator does not know what clean air is; it knows what it was breathing when somebody last zeroed it. Zero it upwind, away from the trench and away from the truck's own exhaust, and hold it long enough for the sensor to come to temperature and the reading to stop walking. An instrument zeroed in a hurry, or zeroed in the sample, produces a survey that proves the street is clear when it is not. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Work out where it can travel before you drive a hole | Find (any order: the backfilled trench over the main, the sewer lateral crossing it, the water service into the building) | Gas leaving a main rarely comes up through the ground directly above the leak. It takes the path of least resistance, which is the trench somebody backfilled with sand, and it stays under whatever is capping the surface until the cap runs out. Reading the street for its utilities first is what tells you where to put the bar holes, and just as importantly which buildings are downstream of a leak that has not reached them yet. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Bar-hole outward, source first | Sequence (strict order: over the main, at the service tee, out along the trench, at the foundation wall) | The pattern is the finding. Driven outward from the suspected source you watch the readings fall away and can say where the gas stops; driven inward from the building you only ever confirm the one hole you were already worried about and never learn the extent. Each hole goes down to the bedding and is angled off the pipe rather than onto it, because a bar driven onto a plastic service is how a survey becomes a second leak. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Read the bar hole in open soil | Graded reading | The reading you write down is the settled one. The first swing after the probe goes in is the air that was already in the hose and the top of the hole; the last is the hole drawing fresh air in around the probe as the pump empties it. Between those two is the plateau, and the plateau is the sample. A reading taken on the way up is a guess, and it is a guess somebody will read months later while deciding whether to dig this street up. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Read the hole at the foundation | Graded reading | This is the reading the grade turns on. The identical deflection that is a scheduled repair out in the carriageway is a different grade entirely once it is against a structure, because a building is a void with people in it and a trench is not. Nothing about the instrument changes here; what changes is that the number now has an address attached to it, and the operator's procedure reads those two facts together rather than either one alone. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | An inside reading — evacuate and call it | Select | At or above the limit that makes it an inside reading, the job changes character: this is no longer a survey to be completed, it is an occupied building with a flammable atmosphere in it. Everybody comes out, nothing electrical is touched on the way — no lights, no switches, no phones used indoors — and the call goes to the emergency number and to gas control together, because what is needed now is a crew and a shutdown rather than a serviceperson with a bar. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Close the approach off | Carry and place | Once the grade is going to be a serious one, everything that can drive, park or wander onto the leak has to be stopped at a distance rather than waved at as it arrives. A traffic control point put in early is also what keeps the next vehicle — a delivery van, a neighbour, the crew who came to help — from parking on the bar holes with its engine running, which is the commonest way a controlled gas call acquires an ignition source. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Shut the service at the curb box | Turn | Shutting the service takes the building out of the equation without touching the main, which is what lets one person make the street safe while the crew is still on the way. It is done at the curb box from the surface with a key, so nothing has to be opened and nobody has to stand at the meter set. It is also the point of no return for the occupants' heating, which is why it happens after they are out and after it has been called in, rather than before either. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Hold a sample on the hole while it is logged | Tracked hold | A logged reading has to be a repeatable one, which means the sample rate on this hole has to match the rate on the last. Squeeze too hard and the aspirator pulls fresh air in around the probe and reports a leak smaller than it is; too slow and the hose never clears, so every hole reads like the one before it. Steady is what makes three holes comparable, and comparable is the entire basis on which an extent gets drawn. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Prove the extent before you hand it over | Find (any order: the outermost hole that reads clean, the manhole on the lateral, the frozen and overlaid cap) | An extent is what turns a reading into a work order. It is the outermost clean hole, the structures and voids the gas could have reached on the way, and the surface condition that explains why it went where it went. Handed over without those, the repair crew arrives knowing only that somewhere near this address there is gas, which is exactly what the caller told the control room hours ago. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 13 | Assign the grade the procedure gives | Select | The grade is not a judgement about how bad it smells or how experienced you are; it is the output of a table the operator wrote and files against, keyed on where the gas was found and what it is near. The same reading in open soil at the kerb and against an occupied foundation produce different grades, different response times and different re-check intervals, and the whole value of writing it this way is that the next person can reproduce your answer from your readings. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 14 | Record it, pass it, and stay with it | Sequence (strict order: readings, sketch and grade recorded, passed to gas control and the repair crew, site left guarded and lit) | The record is the leak's only memory: the readings, the locations, the grade, the sketch of the bar hole pattern and the time each hole was taken. It is what the re-check is measured against and what an investigator reads if this street ever makes the news. Passing it by voice as well as in writing means the crew arrives already knowing the grade, and the site stays guarded because a barricade on its own never stopped the one person who decides the tape does not apply to them. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `doorbell` | You pressed the bell push to warn the householder. A bell push is a switch, and a switch makes a small arc every time it closes — on the outside wall of a building whose foundation hole has just read. The trade knocks. It knocks on the door frame, it leaves the bell, the light switches and the garage opener alone, and it tells the occupants to leave everything electrical exactly as they found it on the way out. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `truck-start` | You reached for the ignition to bring the truck up closer to the work. Your own vehicle is the largest and most reliable ignition source on this street: a starter motor, an alternator, a hot exhaust and a catalytic converter, all of them arriving directly over a trench that is venting. The truck gets parked upwind and clear at the start of the call and it stays there — the last few metres are walked, carrying what is rated to be carried. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `basement-entry` | You went down the cellar steps to find where it is coming in. An inside reading is the one finding on this call that ends the investigation instead of directing it: below grade, in an unventilated space with a standing pilot in the corner of it, there is nothing a serviceperson can usefully learn that is worth being in the room to learn it. Get the occupants out, get yourself out, call it in, and let the reading be taken from the doorway with a probe. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `zero-at-leak` | You zeroed the combustible gas indicator standing over the bar holes. A catalytic instrument zeroes to whatever air it is breathing at the moment you press it, so zeroing it in gas teaches it that gas is zero — and it will then read clean all the way down the street while you walk away from a leak it has been calibrated not to see. The zero is taken upwind, in air you have first proved is clean, and it is taken again after the instrument has been switched off or has sat in a rich sample. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Wind Nacelle (SmartCiti.X · `wind-nacelle`)

**Category:** Energy & Power  
**Trade:** Wind turbine technician  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IBEW / IUOE — utility-scale wind technician; GWO Basic Safety Training (working at height, first aid, manual handling, fire awareness); OSHA 29 CFR 1910.147 for the rotor, yaw and converter locks; 1910.269 for the electrical isolation  
**Tagline:** Gearbox service ninety metres up: rotor lock, yaw lock, converter isolation, oil sampled hot, brake pads gauged, hatch closed  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-21 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Take the turbine work order and the wind limit | Select | Every turbine task carries a wind limit, because above it the nacelle moves, the hatch cannot be controlled and rescue from height stops being possible. The number is on the order and it is checked against the anemometer, not the sky. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Read the nacelle anemometer | Graded reading | Ninety metres up, the wind is not the wind at the base. The nacelle's own anemometer is the one the limit is written against, and it is read at the top before the work starts and again if it freshens. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Three locks: rotor, yaw, converter | Sequence (strict order: rotor lock pin, yaw brake lock, converter isolation) | Three different things can move or energise in here and they are stopped three different ways. The rotor is mechanical, the yaw is hydraulic, the converter is electrical, and one lock does not do the job of the others. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Your lock on the group lock box | Select | The keys to those three locks live in a box that cannot open while your padlock is on it. That is the difference between an isolation somebody else is maintaining and an isolation you control. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Harness, twin lanyard, rated anchor | Sequence (any order: full-body harness, twin lanyard, rated anchor) | Inside a nacelle there is an open hoist hatch and no floor over it. The twin lanyard is so that moving past the hatch never requires being unattached, and the anchor is the one the manufacturer rated, not the nearest pipe. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Open and guard the hoist hatch | Select | The hatch is how the oil and the tools come up, and it is an eighty-five metre hole in the floor for as long as it is open. It gets a rail and it never gets left open unattended. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Check the gearbox oil temperature | Graded reading | Cold oil will not carry the wear particles out and the drain takes an hour. Hot oil comes out as a jet. There is a window between those, and the thermometer says whether you are in it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Set the splash shield | Select | Even inside the window the first of it comes out under head pressure. The shield is between the plug and the person turning it, and it takes two seconds to set. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Crack the drain plug | Turn | Slowly, because a plug that comes out in one turn comes out with the oil behind it. The hose is already connected to the down-tower tank before the plug moves. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Draw the oil sample mid-stream | Select | The first of the flow carries whatever settled in the drain and the last carries the sump floor. The mid-stream sample is the one that represents the oil the gearbox has actually been running on. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Gauge the yaw brake pads | Graded reading | The yaw brake is what holds the nacelle against a wind shift. Pads below the limit let the nacelle walk under load, and a nacelle that walks tears the tower cable loop it is hanging on. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Refit and torque the drain plug | Timed hold | A gearbox drain plug backing out is eight hundred litres of oil down a tower and a machine destroyed. New washer, right figure, held to the click. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 13 | Walk the nacelle before you close it | Find (any order: chafed converter cable, loose coupling guard) | The next person up this tower might be six months away. What gets written down today is what gets fixed before the machine finds it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `unlocked-rotor` | You opened the gearbox with the rotor lock pin not seated. A parked rotor still creeps in gusting wind, and a turning low-speed shaft inside a nacelle takes an arm before anyone can react. The pin goes in and is confirmed visually, every time. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `yaw-free` | You are working with the yaw drive free. The nacelle will hunt into a wind shift with you standing in it, and the tower cable below you twists with it — a yaw event with the hatch open throws tools out and drops them ninety metres. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `unclipped` | You moved inside the nacelle without transferring your lanyard. There is an open hatch in this floor and an eighty-five metre drop under it; the twin lanyard exists so that one end is always attached during the transfer. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `hot-oil` | You cracked the drain at full operating temperature with no shield. Gear oil at ninety degrees under head pressure comes out as a jet, not a trickle, and it goes straight through a coverall. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Transformer Vault (SmartCiti.X · `transformer-vault`)

**Category:** Energy & Power  
**Trade:** Substation / underground distribution electrician  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IBEW — underground distribution journeyman; NFPA 70E arc-flash boundary and PPE category; OSHA 29 CFR 1910.269 for the switching, clearance and grounding; ASTM D3612 dissolved-gas sampling  
**Tagline:** Padmount switching under a written order: clearance, hot stick, load-break elbows, grounds applied, oil drawn for DGA  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-21 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Take the written switching order | Select | Underground loops can be fed from either end. The order says which switch opens first so the vault is fed from nowhere, and it is written down because a switching sequence held in the head is how a loop gets backfed. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Arc-rated PPE and the flash boundary | Sequence (any order: arc-rated suit, dated class 2 gloves, flash boundary markers) | The label on the transformer gives the incident energy and the boundary. The suit is chosen against that number, the gloves are in test date, and the boundary keeps everyone who is not switching outside it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Open and ventilate the vault | Select | A below-grade vault is a permit space. Whatever has collected in it — SF6, exhaust, water vapour off a hot tank — sits at the bottom where a person's head goes first. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Test the vault atmosphere | Graded reading | The meter goes down before the person does, and it reads at the bottom, not at the lip. Twenty point nine oxygen at the top of a vault says nothing about the bottom of it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Open the loop switch to the order | Turn | The loop is opened at the point the order specifies so that the section containing this transformer is fed from neither direction. Opening the wrong end leaves it live from the other. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Park the load-break elbows with the hot stick | Timed hold | A load-break elbow interrupts current as it breaks, which is violent and bright. The stick keeps the length of an insulated pole between that event and the person making it, and the elbow goes on a parking stand rather than lying in the vault. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Test dead on every phase | Graded reading | The tester is proved on a known live source before and after, because a tester that has failed reads dead on everything. Three phases, every time — the one you skip is the one that is still up. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Apply the grounds — earth end first | Sequence (strict order: ground cluster to the grid, phase A, phase B, phase C) | The earth connection is made before the phase connection, every time. Making the phase end first turns the ground set into the conductor that carries fault current through your hands to nothing. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Take your clearance | Select | The clearance is held by the person doing the work, not by the control room's intention. The number on the tag matches the number on the order, and it comes off when the work does. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Draw an oil sample for dissolved-gas analysis | Graded reading | Dissolved-gas analysis reads the transformer's health from what the oil has absorbed — acetylene means arcing, ethylene means overheating. Air in the syringe invalidates the sample, so it is drawn with no bubble. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Remove the grounds — phases first, earth last | Sequence (strict order: phase C, phase B, phase A, ground cluster last) | Coming off, the earth connection is the last thing broken, for the same reason it was the first thing made. Anything else leaves a ground set hanging on a conductor with no path to earth. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Walk the vault before you close it | Find (any order: cracked standoff bushing, oil stain under the tank) | The vault gets opened once a year if it is lucky. What you noticed today is what gets fixed before the next fault finds it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `bare-elbow` | You went for the elbow bare-handed. A 600-amp load-break elbow is an energised connector behind an insulating boot — it is pulled with a hot stick, in rubber gloves, from outside the flash boundary, and never with a hand on the cable. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `no-ground-work` | You started work on the conductor before the grounds were applied. De-energised is not the same as grounded: a backfeed from a customer generator or an induced voltage from the parallel circuit will kill you just as dead, and the grounds are the only thing that holds the conductor at earth. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `vault-entry` | You climbed into the vault before it was tested and ventilated. Below grade with a transformer in it is a permit space — heavier-than-air gases pool in it, and an oil fire consumes the oxygen in seconds. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `wet-gloves` | Those rubber gloves have been sitting in the rain in an open bag. Class 2 gloves are tested, dated and kept in their bag dry; wet or undated gloves are not PPE, they are the illusion of it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

# Mobility & Transit

## Signal Cabinet (SmartCiti.X · `signal-cabinet`)

**Category:** Mobility & Transit  
**Trade:** Traffic signal technician  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IBEW — IMSA Level II Traffic Signal Technician certified  
**Tagline:** Intersection work zone, controller fault diagnosis and conflict monitor integrity  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-21 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Notify the traffic management centre | Select | The centre is watching this intersection remotely through the same detector loops you are about to work on. If they see it drop without warning, dispatch reads it as an outage rather than a scheduled repair and sends a second crew straight into the work zone you have not set up yet. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Set the work zone | Sequence (any order: advance warning sign, taper cone, buffer cone, arrow board) | Traffic needs to be told what is happening far enough back to react to it, which is exactly what the temporary-traffic-control layouts in Part 6 of the MUTCD are built around. The advance sign gives a driver time to notice, the taper is what actually moves them over, and the buffer is what absorbs the one who does not. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | High-visibility clothing | Select | You are a pedestrian in a roadway with a controller cabinet between you and oncoming traffic. Conspicuity, the class-3 garment rated for this kind of exposure, is the only protection you have against a driver who is looking at their phone instead of the cones. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Open the controller cabinet | Select | Now that the zone is up and the centre knows, the cabinet can come open — with the door swung out to put steel between you and the lane, not to make the rack easier to reach. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Put the intersection into flash | Select | Flash puts the intersection into a known, fail-safe state every driver recognises on sight. Working a running controller means one wrong card pull changes a phase under a car that is already committed to the intersection at 50 km/h. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Find the cabinet faults | Find (any order: scorched load switch, corroded ground bond, loose detector loop terminal) | Fault-finding is looking, not swapping — an IBEW-trained signal tech reads the rack the way NECA apprenticeship training teaches it: scorching, corrosion and a backed-out conductor each tell you a different part of the story, and none of them is fixed by trading a healthy part for another healthy part. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Replace the failed load switch | Select | A welded load switch can hold an output on regardless of what the controller commands it to do. Contacts that have arced together do not get reset back into service — they get replaced, because the next command they ignore could be the one that drops a conflicting phase. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Test the conflict monitor | Graded reading | The malfunction monitoring unit watches for conflicting greens on the same intersection and forces the whole thing to flash the instant it sees one. Proving it trips inside its rated window, not just that it powers up, is the entire reason the intersection is allowed back in normal service today. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Set the clearance interval | Graded reading | Clearance timing is calculated from this approach's posted speed and the intersection's width, not chosen by feel. Shave it to improve throughput and you build a dilemma zone: a band of distance where a driver can neither stop in time nor clear the box before opposing traffic gets its green. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Return the intersection to normal | Select | You watch it cycle before you pack up, start to finish, phase by phase. A controller that comes back with the wrong phase order or a stuck call is your fault until you have personally seen it run a complete, correct cycle with your own eyes. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Recover the work zone | Select | The zone comes down from the traffic side inward — arrow board last, not first — so at every point in the teardown you are never the first unprotected thing an approaching driver meets, the way you were on the way in. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `conflict-bypass` | That jumper defeats the conflict monitor. The monitor is the only thing that stops the controller showing green to crossing traffic at the same time. Defeating it is how intersections kill people. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `live-terminals` | You are across the 120 V field terminals with the cabinet live. Those feed the heads outside; the cabinet stays powered even in flash, which is exactly the kind of exposure NFPA 70E's electrical safe work practices are written to control. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `step-into-road` | You stepped into the running lane. Your work zone exists so you never have to — stay behind the cones and let traffic pass. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `ladder-unsecured` | That ladder is footed on a kerb, unsecured, beside a live lane. OSHA's ladder-use rules require it tied off or footed by a second person before anyone climbs it — signal heads get accessed from a properly set ladder or not at all. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Flight Deck (SmartCiti.X · `flight-deck`)

**Category:** Mobility & Transit  
**Trade:** Drone / UAS ground technician  
**Certification / standard claimed:** Teamsters — FAA Part 107 remote pilot certificate holder  
**Tagline:** Vertiport ramp safety: rotor lockout, battery handling, autonomous route planning and pre-flight release  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-21 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Check the flight authorization | Select | Airspace and ramp status change by the hour under 14 CFR Part 107 small-UAS operating rules. Working under an aircraft that the fleet scheduler expects to be flying at 14:20 is how a ground crew ends up standing under a live approach with no warning it is coming. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Set the ground safety zone | Sequence (any order: cone at the ramp edge, cone at the taxi line, ramp barrier) | The zone keeps bystanders off an active ramp and keeps you inside a boundary the flight controller's automated landing logic actually respects — the same OSHA ramp-safety logic that applies to ground crews under any aircraft, crewed or not. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Disarm the flight controller | Select | Disarmed means the motors cannot spin regardless of what a stray signal, a bumped stick or a fleet scheduler's queued mission commands them to do. Nothing touches the airframe before this is confirmed on the link, not assumed from how quiet the rotors look. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Fit the rotor locking pins | Select | Disarmed is a software state that a dispatcher in an office can silently overwrite; the pin is physical and nobody can push a firmware update through it. Both together, not either alone, are what makes an accidental spin-up impossible instead of merely unlikely. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Complete the pre-flight inspection | Find (any order: chipped propeller, cracked motor arm, obstructed obstacle sensor) | A pre-flight inspection is a search, not a checklist tick, and it is the finding the incident report reads first if the release goes ahead without it. Damage that looks cosmetic sitting on the ramp is structural the moment it is loaded at altitude. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Replace the damaged propeller | Carry and place | Matched, balanced propellers keep the airframe's vibration profile inside what the flight controller expects from its accelerometers. A chipped blade throws that profile off just enough that the controller starts compensating for damage instead of flying the mission. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Inspect and fit the flight battery | Select | The battery bay is the last thing on the airframe that opens, and only after the aircraft cannot spin a motor even if you drop a tool into it. A swollen LiPo cell is already venting internally, and NFPA's guidance on lithium battery hazards treats that as a fire risk the instant it is found, not a defect to fly around. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Verify cell voltage balance | Graded reading | A pack with one cell drifting low will sag under load exactly when the aircraft needs full power to land, not while it is cruising at altitude with margin to spare. Balance is checked before every single flight, not just at charge time when the pack was last on the bench. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Contain the damaged battery | Select | A venting lithium cell in a normal storage bin can ignite everything stacked around it within seconds. The fireproof bag, the same NFPA-recommended containment used across the trade for damaged lithium packs, buys the minutes it takes to get it somewhere it can vent safely. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Remove the rotor pins | Select | The pins are the last thing an IAM-trained ground technician does at the aircraft itself. Everything after this — the route, the upload, the verification — happens at the console, so nothing puts a hand near the airframe again between the pins coming out and the release being signed. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Plan the autonomous delivery route | Sequence (strict order: launch waypoint, relay waypoint, delivery waypoint) | The flight controller flies the waypoints in the exact order they were entered, not the order that looks correct on the map. Swap the relay and the delivery point and the aircraft plans a straight line through restricted airspace instead of the corridor around it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Upload the mission | Select | A planned route sitting on the tablet is not a flight plan yet — it is a drawing. Uploading it is the step that actually replaces whatever the aircraft was flying before with the route you just built and checked. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 13 | Verify the route against restricted airspace | Timed hold | Airspace restrictions shift by the hour under Part 107 operating rules, so a corridor that was clear when the route was drawn an hour ago may not be clear now. You verify the specific route you are about to fly, every single time, not the general area it happens to pass through. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 14 | Sign the pre-flight release | Select | The release is a signed record that one named, IAM-certified technician personally checked specific things on this specific airframe today. It is the document an incident investigation reads first if something goes wrong in the air twenty minutes from now. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `spinning-rotor` | You reached toward a rotor with the flight controller still armed. A carbon propeller at flight RPM will not stop for a hand — the aircraft is disarmed and the props are pinned before anyone approaches. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `swollen-battery` | That battery pack is swollen. A puffed LiPo cell is already venting internally; charging or flying it is how a ramp fire starts. It goes straight into the fireproof containment bag. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `ramp-incursion` | You crossed into the active approach path while an aircraft is inbound. The ramp boundary exists because a returning drone is looking for a clear landing pad, not for a person standing on it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `damaged-prop` | That propeller has a chip out of the leading edge. A damaged prop at altitude changes its balance and can shear — it is swapped before the aircraft goes anywhere. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Track Access (SmartCiti.X · `track-access`)

**Category:** Mobility & Transit  
**Trade:** Rail track worker  
**Certification / standard claimed:** BMWED — FRA 49 CFR 214 Roadway Worker Protection qualified  
**Tagline:** Track possession, third-rail isolation and lookout protection  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-21 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Attend the possession briefing | Select | Everyone on site works to the same plan under FRA roadway worker protection rules at 49 CFR Part 214. A worker who missed the briefing does not know where the protection actually starts and ends, and the crew has no way to know that they do not know. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Take possession from the signaller | Select | Until the signaller says the words that grant it, the section is still open to traffic as far as the signalling system is concerned, regardless of what the engineering schedule says should be happening. Possession is a spoken confirmation you can quote back, not an assumption drawn from a timetable. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Isolate the traction current | Turn | The isolator is what removes the current, but the switch position alone is not proof of anything — that comes at the next step. It goes fully over, too: a lever left part way across its travel is a lever that has not actually broken the circuit, whatever the handle looks like from the cab. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Prove the traction rail is dead | Graded reading | The same rule as any other isolation: measured with an approved tester, never assumed from a switch position or a control-room readout. Seven hundred volts DC through a conductor rail gives no warning and needs no arc to kill, so a reading near zero is the only thing that makes the rail approachable at all. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Mark the limits of possession | Sequence (any order: limit of possession board — up end, limit of possession board — down end) | The boards are the only thing a train crew, a signaller or another gang can actually see from a moving vehicle that marks where your protection begins and ends — everything past a board is a section that still runs to normal rules. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Post the lookout | Select | The lookout's only job is watching the line in both directions and giving a warning early enough for the gang to clear it, coordinated the same way a SMART-represented train crew coordinates a move through a work zone. Sighting distance is calculated back from line speed, not chosen for convenience. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Fit the rail bonding clamp | Select | The bond maintains the signalling circuit's continuity across the joint you are about to work on, so the interlocking upstream still sees an occupied or clear track correctly instead of losing the detection the moment the rail is broken. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Measure track gauge | Graded reading | Standard gauge is held to a tight tolerance for a reason: outside it, a wheel flange stops following the rail head and starts climbing it under load, which is how a slow, boring maintenance defect turns into a derailment nobody saw building. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Replace the defective fastener | Select | A loose fastener lets the rail move under load. Fixed now, it is a five-minute job; missed, it is a derailment risk. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Complete the tool tally | Select | A tool left on the track is a foreign object under the next train. The tally is counted out and counted back, every time. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Restore traction current | Select | Confirmed clear of the rail, then and only then re-energised. The sequence never reverses. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Hand possession back | Select | The section stays under your protection until you formally hand it back. Walking away without the call is how a possession never actually closes. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `third-rail` | That is the traction current rail. Isolation confirmed elsewhere does not matter here until you have proven this section dead — it carries enough current to kill on contact, no arc required. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `unprotected-crossing` | You crossed the running line without the lookout's clearance. A train at line speed covers that distance faster than you can react to a warning. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `solo-worksite` | You are working without a second person in sight. Track protection is never a one-person job — the lookout exists because you cannot watch the line and do the work at the same time, and an ANSI/ISEA-rated hi-vis vest does nothing for a worker that nobody is actually watching for. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `unmarked-boundary` | That limit of possession has no board. Without a marked boundary a train movement has no way to know where your protection actually ends. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Bus Depot Lift (SmartCiti.X · `bus-depot-lift`)

**Category:** Mobility & Transit  
**Trade:** Transit bus technician — electric fleet  
**Certification / standard claimed:** ATU / IAM — ASE Transit Bus (H series) with H8 EV / hybrid-electric; ALI Lifting It Right (ANSI/ALI ALOIM); OSHA 1910.147 lockout for high-voltage disable  
**Tagline:** Electric bus on column lifts: HV disable and prove, chocks, pad placement, synchronised raise, locks down, under-bus work, controlled lower  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-21 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the repair order and the lift points | Select | Every model lifts at different frame points and disables its high-voltage system a different way — a point that is correct on a diesel cousin can be sheet metal on this one. The diagram and the disable procedure for this specific bus are read before anything is touched. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Disable the high-voltage system | Sequence (strict order: master switch, HV service disconnect) | The service disconnect opens the traction battery circuit itself, not just the low-voltage control loop the master switch runs. It is pulled after the master switch, in that order, and it stays in your pocket so nobody can reinsert it while you are underneath a bus you believe is de-energised. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Prove the HV bus is de-energised | Graded reading | The traction battery's capacitors hold a lethal charge for a period after the disconnect is pulled, following the same live-dead-live discipline NFPA 70E requires of any electrical energy source. The meter, read only after the full wait, is the actual proof the orange cables are safe to touch. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Chock the wheels | Sequence (any order: front chocks, rear chocks) | The bus must not roll on the lift pads during the first few inches of travel, when the columns have barely started to take the load and the pads have the least purchase on the frame. Chocks at both axles and the parking brake set before any column moves at all. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Place the lift pads on the frame points | Carry and place | The frame lift point is engineered by the manufacturer to carry the full weight of an 18-tonne bus; nothing else along that frame or body is. The pad goes exactly on the marked point, on every column, with no column left to guesswork. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Raise in sync | Tracked hold | Four columns raising even slightly unevenly twist the bus's frame and shift its whole load onto only two of the four pads. The controller works to sync them, but the technician watches the level readout the entire climb and stops the raise the moment one column leads. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Lower onto the mechanical locks | Timed hold | The bus is worked under only once it is resting on its mechanical locks, not on the columns' hydraulic rams. Lowering onto the locks takes the load off the hydraulics entirely, so a seal or a hose failure afterward has nothing left under it to drop. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Work under the bus | Select | Everything before this step — HV proven dead, chocks set, pads on the frame, locks engaged — is what turns pulling a drive motor from underneath an 18-tonne bus into an ordinary task instead of a gamble. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Inspect under the bus while it is up | Find (any order: chafed HV cable jacket) | A bus sits up on locks with clear access underneath for maybe an hour a month, and that hour is the only chance anyone gets to actually see the underside. What gets caught now is what keeps this bus out of the shop again next month. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Clear the bay and lower | Select | The lower is called only by the person who has personally looked underneath the bus and confirmed the bay is clear of hands, tools and feet. The service disconnect goes back in only once the bus is back down on the ground and the job is fully closed. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `under-hydraulic` | You went under the bus with the columns still on hydraulics, locks not engaged. A seal, a hose or a valve is all that is holding 18 tonnes over you; the mechanical locks are what a person works under. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `hv-bare` | You touched the orange high-voltage cable with bare hands before the system was disabled and proven. The traction battery is 600 V and does not care that the bus is switched off. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `pad-on-body` | You put a lift pad on the body skirt instead of the frame lift point. The skirt is sheet metal; it crushes, the bus shifts on the columns, and it comes down where it likes. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `raise-no-chock` | You started the raise with the wheels unchocked and the bus in neutral. A bus that rolls on the pads as it lifts rolls off them. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Airport Ramp (SmartCiti.X · `airport-ramp`)

**Category:** Mobility & Transit  
**Trade:** Airline ramp agent / ground handler  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IAM and Transport Workers Union ramp locals; IATA Ground Operations Manual (AHM 630 ground support equipment, ERA safety envelope); FAA 14 CFR 139.303 personnel training and 139.329 movement-area safety; OSHA 29 CFR 1910.178 for powered ramp equipment  
**Tagline:** Gate turn: FOD walk and equipment restraint line, marshal onto the lead-in, chocks before anything touches it, cones set, ground power and headset to the flight deck, bridge to the door, loader at walking pace, all-clear only when chocks and cones are back  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-21 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the turn plan | Select | Stand geometry and aircraft type decide where the chocks and cones go and how far the bridge travels. The turn is planned on the ground before the aircraft is on final. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Walk the stand for FOD | Find (any order: loose bolt on the stand, cargo strap on the lead-in line) | Foreign object damage is the most expensive avoidable thing on a ramp. The walk happens before every arrival, and what is found goes in the bin, not kicked aside. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Stage behind the line | Sequence (any order: equipment restraint line clear, ground equipment behind the line, hi-vis and ear protection) | Until the aircraft is stopped and chocked, the restraint line is the boundary between the ramp crew and a moving aeroplane. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Marshal the aircraft in | Tracked hold | The pilot cannot see the nosewheel or the wingtips. The marshaller's rate and position are the only reference for the last thirty metres. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Stop on the mark | Graded reading | The stop bar is where the bridge reaches the door and the wingtip clears the stand next door. A metre long or short and one of those is no longer true. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Chock and cone | Sequence (strict order: nose gear chocks, main gear chocks, wingtip and engine cones) | Chocks before contact is the rule the whole ramp runs on. The cones mark the parts of the aircraft that ground equipment must never find with a mirror. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Ground power and headset | Sequence (any order: ground power connected, headset to the flight deck) | Ground power lets the engines and the auxiliary unit shut down. The headset is the only direct line to the flight deck for the rest of the turn, and it is what pushback will happen on. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Drive the bridge to the door | Tracked hold | A bridge driven fast into an aircraft door is a hull strike that cancels the next four flights. Steady, watching the sill, with the auto-level following. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Align and dock the bridge | Graded reading | The canopy seals to the fuselage and the auto-level keeps the sill matched as the aircraft rises with unloading. Off-centre, the seal leaks and the sill steps. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Bring the belt loader to the hold | Select | Everything that approaches an aircraft does it at walking pace and stops short. The last metre is done with the platform, not with the vehicle. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Clear the stand and give the all-clear | Sequence (strict order: cones back on the truck, chocks pulled, all-clear to the flight deck) | The chocks come up second to last and the all-clear last, with the chocks visible to the flight deck. That signal is the pilot's proof that nothing is still attached to their aircraft. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `cross-engine-arc` | You walked through the engine's intake arc with the engine turning. A running narrow-body engine ingests a person from several metres in front of it, and the exhaust behind it will blow a baggage cart across the ramp. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `vehicle-past-line` | You drove ground equipment past the equipment-restraint line before the aircraft stopped and was chocked. Every year a piece of ground equipment strikes a moving aircraft on a ramp; the line exists to make that impossible. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `chock-pulled-early` | You pulled the chocks with the bridge still on the aircraft. Nothing holds the aircraft then except its brakes, and a brake release with the bridge attached takes the door off. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `under-fuselage-loading` | You stood under the fuselage while the loader was moving against it. The loader's platform is a shear point against the aircraft skin, and there is no room to get out from under it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
