# Construction & Structural Trades

## Steel Erector (SmartCiti.X · `steel-erector`)

**Category:** Construction & Structural Trades  
**Trade:** Ironworker / structural steel connector  
**Certification / standard claimed:** Ironworkers — OSHA 29 CFR 1926 Subpart R qualified connector  
**Tagline:** Structural steel connecting: fall protection, tag-line control and the bolt-up sequence  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-21 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the erection plan | Select | The raising gang works one pick at a time from a sequence the engineer set for a reason — steel goes up out of order and the structure is not stable for the next pick. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Connect to the horizontal lifeline | Select | The lifeline runs the length of the beam you are about to walk. Nothing else on a structural floor is rated to catch you before it exists. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Inspect the harness and positioning lanyard | Select | A connector's harness gets checked at the start of every shift it is worn on steel — this is not equipment you find out has failed after it already has. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Confirm crane signal protocol | Select | The operator cannot see the connection point from the cab. Every signal that moves the load has to mean the same thing to everyone on the pick or the load moves on a guess. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Scan the deck for hazards | Find (any order: unmarked deck opening, cracked temporary plank, loose decking sheet) | A deck hazard scan happens before you commit to walking a section, because the deck that looked finished from the ladder is not the same thing as a deck that has actually been checked. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Cover and barricade the opening | Carry and place | A found hazard that is not corrected is just a hazard you now feel less responsible for. The cover goes on before anyone else reaches that section of deck. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Guide the suspended column with the tag line | Timed hold | The tag line keeps your hands a rope's length from a ton of moving steel while still giving you enough control to place it precisely. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Land the member on the splice plate | Select | The splice plate is machined to take the column's base exactly once it is aligned — forcing a mismatched landing bends the plate before the first bolt goes in. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Plumb and align the column | Graded reading | Every connection above this one inherits whatever lean you accept here. Plumb now is the only point in the sequence where it is still cheap to fix. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Install bolts in the required order | Sequence (strict order: temporary bolt A, temporary bolt B, erection bolt) | Temporary bolts hold the connection snug while it is checked and plumbed. The erection bolt goes in last, once the joint is already stable enough that adding it does not shift the alignment. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Apply final torque | Select | Final torque only happens once the minimum number of bolts is in and the connection is confirmed plumb — it is the last thing that happens, never a shortcut to skip the temporary bolts. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Log the connection complete | Select | The crane hook does not come off until the connection is logged as self-supporting. The record is what the inspector and the next raising gang inherit. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `suspended-load-path` | You are standing under the load path. A suspended steel member has nothing holding it up but a sling and a hook — if either lets go, you are standing exactly where it lands. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `open-floor-hole` | That deck opening has no cover and no barricade. On a structural floor, a leading edge you cannot see coming is exactly how a fall happens on a surface that looks solid from three steps away. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `hand-guided-member` | You grabbed the swinging member with your hand instead of the tag line. A ton of suspended steel does not stop when your grip does — it keeps moving and takes your hand, or you, with it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `unclipped-leading-edge` | You stepped to the leading edge without your lanyard connected to the overhead lifeline. At height, the edge of unfinished decking is a fall with nothing between you and the ground below. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

| Check | Result | Note |
|---|---|---|

## Crane Yard (SmartCiti.X · `crane-yard`)

**Category:** Construction & Structural Trades  
**Trade:** Mobile crane operator / rigger  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IUOE — NCCCO Mobile Crane Operator certified  
**Tagline:** Mobile crane pick: outrigger setup, load chart verification, anti-collision zone programming and a tag-line controlled lift  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-21 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the lift plan | Select | Every control that follows is sized to this specific pick, and OSHA's cranes rule at 29 CFR 1926 subpart CC puts the plan on the crew before the machine is set, not on the office afterwards. A lift plan read once the outriggers are already down is a plan you are checking your own work against. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Extend and set the outriggers | Sequence (any order: front outriggers, rear outriggers) | Rated capacity is published for beams fully extended onto adequate blocking. Retract them part way and the tipping axis moves in toward the boom, so ASME B30.5 treats that as a different chart entirely — one most cabs do not carry. The outriggers are what turn a number on paper into something this ground can hold. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Check the crane is level | Graded reading | A crane that is off level derates in the direction of the lean without saying so. Manufacturers publish their charts for a machine level within about one percent of grade, and a long boom leaning three degrees can shed a double-digit percentage of its rating — this is where you prove it rather than assume it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Verify the load chart for this pick | Graded reading | The chart is read at the actual radius and boom angle for this pick, not the radius from last week's job that felt about the same. Capacity falls away faster than radius grows, so walking the boom out a few feet can cost a fifth of the rating — and under 29 CFR 1926 subpart CC the chart, not the operator's feel for it, is the limit. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Teach the anti-collision boundary | Sequence (strict order: boundary corner A, boundary corner B, boundary corner C) | The RCI's anti-collision system only knows about a boundary you actually taught it, and it joins the points in the order it received them. Teach the corners out of order and it draws the line through the wrong side of the property — the boom then stops where nothing is and swings freely through the place you thought was fenced. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Save the boundary to the RCI | Select | A taught boundary sitting unsaved is just where the boom happened to point, and it is gone the next time the machine is keyed off. Saving it is what makes the rated capacity indicator actually cut the swing at that line, instead of leaving it to an operator remembering a property line at the end of a twelve-hour shift. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Test-swing and verify the cutout | Timed hold | An operational aid is only a control once it has been proved to act, which is why ASME B30.5 has you function-test limiting devices rather than assume them. Verifying a brand-new zone at slow test speed means the first time it stops the boom is a test, not a pick with a load hanging off the hook. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Select the correctly rated sling | Select | A sling's tag capacity assumes it is undamaged. ASME B30.9 requires an inspection before every use and lists kinks, broken stitching, heat damage and a missing tag as remove-from-service conditions — the rack always has more than one option, and the rated, sound one is the only correct choice. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Inspect the shackle | Select | A shackle with a worn or bent pin can walk loose under a cyclical load, and ASME B30.26 puts the removal criterion at roughly a tenth of the original diameter worn off the pin or the bow. The stamp tells you what it was rated for; the inspection tells you whether it still is. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Measure the sling leg angle | Graded reading | Leg tension is the share of the load divided by the sine of the angle from horizontal. Two legs at 60 degrees each carry about 58 percent of the load; at 45 they carry 71 percent, and at 30 each leg is carrying the whole load on its own. ASME B30.9 is why the angle gets measured, not eyeballed. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Confirm the signal person protocol | Select | One qualified signal person directs the lift and the operator moves on that person's signal alone — with one exception written into 29 CFR 1926 subpart CC: a stop signal from anybody on the site has to be obeyed. Everyone on the pick needs to know who is calling it before the load leaves the ground. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Hook the rigging to the load | Select | The hook has to come over the load's centre of gravity before anything is taken up. A load hooked off centre swings the instant it clears the ground and puts a side pull into a boom rated only for a vertical one — and that is the moment you have least control over either. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 13 | Guide the lift with the tag line | Tracked hold | A tag line steers a suspended load without anybody putting a hand on it, and it only works at a steady light tension. Haul on it and you drag the load off plumb into a side load the boom is not rated for; let it go slack and the load keeps whatever swing it already had until something stops it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 14 | Make a controlled set-down | Carry and place | A controlled set-down means the load is sitting flat and taking its own weight before any rigging comes off. Lower it fast and the slings go slack under something that is still rocking, and the rigger who steps in to unhook is doing it under a load that can still shift onto them. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 15 | Close out the lift log | Select | The lift is not finished when the load lands. What was picked, at what radius, and anything found on the pre-use inspection are the records 29 CFR 1926 subpart CC expects to exist, and they are what the next crew and the next annual inspection work from. Rigging recovered, area confirmed clear, then logged. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `swing-radius-stand` | You are standing inside the crane's swing radius while it is rigged for a pick. The counterweight sweeps that space at walking pace with no warning a person on foot can react to, which is why OSHA's cranes rule at 29 CFR 1926 subpart CC makes barriering the swing radius the employer's job rather than the pedestrian's. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `two-block-risk` | You are hoisting past the anti-two-block limit. Running the hook block into the boom tip parts the hoist line or buckles the tip section, and the load falls the moment either one lets go — which is why ANSI/ASME B30.5 and the OSHA rule both put an anti-two-block device on the required list rather than the optional one. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `chart-exceeded` | That pick is logged past the chart's rated capacity for this radius and boom angle. A mobile crane's chart already has its safety factor built in, so the plate is the limit and not a target: cranes tip or fail structurally at the edge of the chart, not with a comfortable margin of warning first. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `kinked-sling` | That sling has a visible kink in its body. A kink is a stress concentration the sling was never rated for, and ASME B30.9 lists it as a remove-from-service condition precisely because the sling lets go at the kink well under its tag capacity. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Trench Box (SmartCiti.X · `trench-box`)

**Category:** Construction & Structural Trades  
**Trade:** Laborer / excavation and shoring  
**Certification / standard claimed:** LIUNA — OSHA 29 CFR 1926 Subpart P Competent Person  
**Tagline:** Excavation shoring: competent-person inspection, atmosphere testing and protective systems  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-21 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Guard the excavation | Sequence (any order: cone at the approach, cone at the far end, trench guard rail) | An open trench next to foot traffic or vehicle movement is a hazard to everyone who is not on this crew, guarded before the first assessment even starts. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Read the excavation permit | Select | The permit sets the protective system for this trench from its soil classification and depth, not from what looked adequate on the last job. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Complete the competent-person inspection | Select | A competent person checks the actual walls in front of them every day conditions could have changed — after rain, after a freeze-thaw, after any nearby vibration. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Set the spoil pile back from the edge | Select | Spoil piled at the edge adds surcharge load exactly where the wall is already carrying the most stress. Setting it back removes that extra weight from the equation entirely. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Test the trench atmosphere | Graded reading | Tested before every entry, not just the first one of the day — a nicked gas line or a change in groundwater can turn a trench's atmosphere hours after it was last checked. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Place the trench box | Carry and place | The box is rated for this depth and soil class from the permit. It goes in before entry, not as a precaution added after someone is already working below grade. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Place the access ladder | Carry and place | A ladder within 25 feet means an exit is never more than a few steps away, in the trench or on the surface, in an emergency that gives you no time to walk further. Where it ends up is the whole rule, so putting it there is the step. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Maintain contact with the spotter | Timed hold | The spotter watches the walls, the spoil pile and the surrounding area the whole time you cannot, because your attention below grade is on the pipe, not the edge. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Complete the utility line repair | Select | The actual task only starts once guarding, inspection, atmosphere and shoring are all already in place — not run in parallel with them to save time. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Teach the GPS grade-control waypoints | Sequence (strict order: Start waypoint, Mid waypoint, End waypoint) | The automated grader follows these points in the order they're recorded, not the order that seems obvious — teach them start to end, matching the pipe's actual slope. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Save the grade-control profile | Select | An untaught point list is just recorded positions. Saving it is what turns three grade points into a profile the grader can actually run against. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Dry-run the grade-control profile | Timed hold | A brand-new profile is verified at a walk-through pace with a hand on the console, watching the whole run, before the grader ever moves unattended over a trench a crew just worked in. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 13 | Take a headcount before backfill | Select | A headcount against the crew list is what confirms the trench is actually empty — not an assumption because the ladder looked clear from the surface. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 14 | Remove the protective box | Carry and place | The box comes out only after everyone is confirmed clear of the trench — removing protection with anyone still below grade defeats the entire point of having placed it. It comes out the way it went in, lifted clear and set down where it is not in anybody's road. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 15 | Backfill and compact | Select | Backfilling in compacted lifts is what keeps the surface from settling later and undoing the repair that was just made underneath it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `unshored-entry` | You stepped into the trench before the protective system was placed. A cubic metre of soil weighs more than a small car, and an unprotected wall can let go with no warning at all before it happens. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `spoil-too-close` | That spoil pile is sitting right on the edge. The extra surcharge load from a pile that close is exactly what pushes a marginal trench wall past the point it can hold itself up. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `no-ladder-access` | There is no ladder within reach of this section. If the wall lets go or the atmosphere turns, everyone below grade needs an exit inside seconds, not a walk to find one. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `untested-entry` | You are entering before the atmosphere has been tested. A trench can fill with oxygen-deficient or toxic air from a nicked utility line with no smell and no colour to warn you first. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Scaffold Erection (SmartCiti.X · `scaffold-erection`)

**Category:** Construction & Structural Trades  
**Trade:** Scaffold erector / carpenter  
**Certification / standard claimed:** UBC (Carpenters) — scaffold erector qualification; OSHA 29 CFR 1926.451 / 1926.454 scaffold competent person; fall protection during erection per 1926.451(g)  
**Tagline:** Frame scaffold, two lifts: ground and sills, base plates, plumb and level, tie-ins, full planking, guardrails, ladder access, green tag  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-21 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the scaffold plan | Select | The plan is the competent person's design: how high, how tied, how loaded. The erection follows it; it does not improvise on site. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Inspect the ground | Select | Everything above the sills stands on this. Soft ground under one leg settles under load, and the scaffold racks toward it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Set the mudsills and base plates | Sequence (strict order: left mudsill, right mudsill, base plates on the sills) | The sill spreads the leg load over the ground; the base plate sits on the sill, never on the ground or a block. In that order, so the jacks have something to stand on. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Stand the first lift | Carry and place | The first lift sets everything above it. It goes on the plates square, braced both sides, before anyone lets go of it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Plumb and level the first lift | Graded reading | Out of plumb at the first lift is doubled at the second. The jacks are adjusted now, while the scaffold is one lift high and light. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Tie the scaffold to the structure | Timed hold | The tie is what makes the scaffold part of the building instead of a tower beside it. It goes in at the first lift, before the second goes up. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Stand the second lift | Carry and place | The second lift goes on a plumb, level, tied first lift. Pins in so the frames cannot lift off; braces on so they cannot rack. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Fully plank the working platform | Sequence (strict order: outside plank, middle plank, wall-side plank) | Full planking is the floor the crew works on. A gap is a leg through the platform; an unsecured plank is a plank that walks off the ledger. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Guardrails and toeboards | Sequence (any order: top rail, mid rail, toeboard) | Ten feet up, the guardrail is the fall protection. Top rail stops a person, mid rail stops a slip under it, toeboard stops the hammer going over onto whoever is below. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Fit the access ladder | Select | The ladder is how the scaffold is used, not just built. Without it, the first person up climbs the braces — and so does everyone after. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Competent person inspection | Find (any order: missing coupling pin) | The tag is the competent person's word that the scaffold is safe. The inspection is the work behind the word. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Hang the green tag | Select | Green says built and inspected; anyone may use it. Yellow says restrictions; red says stay off. The colour at the ladder is the whole crew's instruction. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `climb-frames` | You climbed the cross-braces to reach the second lift. Braces are not a ladder; they flex, they are not spaced for feet, and the fall is onto the frames below. Access is a ladder or a stair, built in. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `untied-work` | You started planking the second lift with no tie to the structure. Two lifts of frame scaffold with no tie is a free-standing tower over its height-to-base ratio — the wind, or one person leaning, is enough. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `block-on-sill` | You set a base plate on a concrete block instead of a mudsill. Blocks crack and tip; a base plate on a tipped block puts a leg in the air and the load on the other three. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `plank-gap` | You left a plank gap over the wall side wide enough to step through. Full planking means no gap over 1 inch at the wall, and none anywhere a foot can go. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Concrete Pour (SmartCiti.X · `concrete-pour`)

**Category:** Construction & Structural Trades  
**Trade:** Cement mason / concrete finisher and laborer  
**Certification / standard claimed:** OPCMIA cement masons and LIUNA laborers; ACI Concrete Field Testing Technician Grade I (slump ASTM C143, air C231, cylinders C31); OSHA 29 CFR 1926 Subpart Q concrete and masonry (1926.701 impalement protection, 1926.703 formwork); ACI 347 formwork pressure and OSHA 1926.1153 respirable crystalline silica  
**Tagline:** Wall pour from a boom pump: pre-pour walk, rebar capped and forms braced, slump and air tested before the first yard, nobody under the boom, placement rate inside the form pressure, vibrated not segregated, cylinders cast, finish on the clock  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-21 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the pour card | Select | Formwork is engineered for a rate of rise, not a volume. The card carries that number, the mix, and the temperature window the pour is allowed in. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Walk the pour before the truck arrives | Find (any order: uncapped vertical rebar, unpinned form brace, debris in the form) | Everything found now is a five-minute fix. The same things found once concrete is moving are a stopped pour, a cold joint, or somebody in the ambulance. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Correct what the walk found | Sequence (any order: rebar caps fitted, brace pinned, form blown out) | A defect found and not fixed is worse than one never found, because now the crew believes the walk was done. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Slump test the first truck | Graded reading | Slump is the check that the mix on site is the mix that was ordered. Too stiff will not fill the form; too wet has had water added on site and has lost the strength the engineer specified. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Air content and cylinders | Sequence (strict order: air content measured, test cylinders cast) | Entrained air is what survives a freeze-thaw winter, and the cylinders are the only proof of strength anyone will have in twenty-eight days. Both are taken from the same load, before placement. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Clear the boom zone | Select | The zone under a charged line is the one place on a pour nobody has a reason to be. A plug clears as a whip, and it does it when the pressure finally breaks it, not when you are watching. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Place the first lift | Tracked hold | The rate of rise is what sets the pressure on the form. Placing fast in one corner is how a wall form blows, and it is the most common formwork failure there is. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Consolidate the lift | Timed hold | Vibration drives out entrapped air; over-vibration drives the aggregate down and the paste up, which is segregation. Straight in, straight out, and never used to move concrete sideways. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Set the construction joint key | Turn | The key is what makes the next lift act as one wall with this one. It goes in while the concrete is still plastic, and the surface is roughened and cleaned before the next pour. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Wait out the bleed water | Graded reading | Finishing while bleed water is on the surface works that water back into the top, and the top is the part that has to last. This is the single most common way a slab is ruined. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Float, edge and cure | Sequence (strict order: bull float, edger and jointer, curing applied) | Curing is not cleanup, it is the last structural step: concrete that dries instead of curing loses a large part of its strength and crazes on the surface. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Cut the hardened spill wet | Carry and place | Cutting concrete dry is a silica exposure many times over the limit. Water on the blade or a vacuum shroud is not optional, and it is the difference between a nuisance and silicosis. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `under-the-boom` | You stood under the pump boom with the line charged. A plugged line clears as a whip: the hose end swings hard enough to kill, and the plug lets go without warning when the pressure finally breaks it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `uncapped-rebar` | You worked over uncapped vertical rebar. A fall of any height onto an uncapped bar is an impalement; the caps are rated for it and they cost less than the ambulance. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `overfill-forms` | You kept placing after the forms started to bow. Formwork fails all at once, not gradually, and it buries whoever is inside the pour line under tonnes of wet concrete. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `dry-cut-silica` | You dry-cut the hardened spill with no water and no vacuum. Cutting concrete dry puts respirable crystalline silica in the air at many times the permissible limit, and silicosis does not get better. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Post Tension (SmartCiti.X · `post-tension`)

**Category:** Construction & Structural Trades  
**Trade:** Ironworker — post-tensioning crew  
**Certification / standard claimed:** Ironworkers (IW) reinforcing and post-tensioning crews; PTI Level 1 and Level 2 certification for unbonded post-tensioning field personnel; ACI 318 for the compressive strength the concrete must reach before the tendons are stressed; OSHA 29 CFR 1926 Subpart Q concrete and masonry construction — 1926.701(c) post-tensioning: nobody but the crew essential to the operation behind the jack, and signs and barriers limiting access to the area during tensioning  
**Tagline:** Stressing tendons in a cast-in-place slab: strength proven before transfer, calibrated jack and gauge, both end cones barricaded and nobody behind the ram, force ramped, elongation measured against the record, and nothing cut until the engineer accepts it  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-21 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Take the stressing record and the tendon schedule | Select | The stressing record is the drawing for this operation and the document the job is accepted on. Every number that decides whether the tendon is right — force, calculated elongation, the tolerance that elongation is judged against — is on it before anyone touches a pump. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Prove the concrete has reached stressing strength | Graded reading | Concrete is stressed on a strength result, never on how many days it has been. ACI 318 requires the specified compressive strength at transfer before the tendons take load, and cylinders or a maturity meter are how that is known — a slab poured in cold weather can be days behind the calendar and look identical. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Check the jack and gauge calibration | Select | The gauge is the only thing telling you how hard you are pulling, and it only means anything as half of the pair it was calibrated with. A gauge off its ram, or a certificate out of date, turns the whole operation into a guess — and the elongation check downstream cannot tell you which of the two lied. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Inspect both ends before anything is pressurised | Find (any order: grout in the wedge cavity, split sheathing at the anchorage, honeycombed concrete behind a bearing plate) | Every one of these is a five-minute job now and an anchorage failure later. The anchorage is the only part of a tendon anybody can see, so it is the only part that gets inspected — which is exactly why it has to be inspected properly. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Barricade both cones, live end first | Sequence (strict order: cone behind the live end, cone off the dead end, signs on the approaches) | The exclusion zone for a stressing operation is a cone off each end of the tendon, because that is the direction the energy leaves in. Live end first: that is where the jack is going and where somebody is about to be working. The signs go up last because they are what keep everybody who is not on this crew out of both. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Mark the strand for the elongation measurement | Select | Elongation is a difference between two positions of the same mark, so the mark is made before any load is on the tendon. No mark, no measurement, and no way of knowing afterwards whether this tendon got the force the engineer designed for. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Mount the jack on the tendon | Carry and place | A jack that is not square on the plate loads the anchorage on one side and can jump off it under pressure. Seating it properly is also the last work anybody does at that end of the tendon — from here on the operation is run from beside it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Put the crew at right angles and call the zone clear | Select | Beside the tendon is the only place to stand: ninety degrees to the axis, out of both cones, where you can see the ram and the gauge. The call is not a courtesy — it is how the person who wandered onto your deck finds out that the slab in front of them is about to be loaded. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Ramp the jack to the specified force | Tracked hold | The force comes up smoothly and stops at the figure on the record. Snatching at it overshoots a tendon there is no way of un-stressing, and stopping short leaves a slab that is not carrying what it was designed to carry — and both of those are read afterwards, off the elongation. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Measure the elongation against the record | Graded reading | This is the proof. The measured elongation is compared with the elongation calculated on the stressing record, and a reading outside the range that record accepts stops the operation: it means the tendon is binding somewhere in the slab, the jack and gauge are out, or the strand in the duct is not the strand on the drawing. Which of those it is, is the engineer's to work out. The seven per cent on this record is the discrepancy at which ACI 318 requires the cause to be found rather than argued about. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Seat the wedges at the design force | Timed hold | The wedges are what hold the tendon for the next eighty years, and they are set hydraulically, at full force, in one movement. Wedges that are not fully home let the strand draw back through them, and the slab quietly loses the force the record says it has. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Release the pressure and retract the ram | Turn | The anchorage takes the load at the moment the jack gives it up, and that is when a badly seated wedge announces itself. The pressure comes off slowly, from the side, with the cone still empty — the barricades come down after the jack does, not before. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 13 | Record and accept, then cut and grout | Sequence (strict order: elongations accepted, tails cut, pocket grouted) | In that order, because cutting is irreversible: a tendon with its tail cut off can never be re-stressed or re-measured, so nothing is cut until the engineer has the numbers and has taken them. Grout goes in last and seals the anchorage against the water that would corrode it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 14 | Walk the stressed tendons before the deck is handed over | Find (any order: wedge that has drawn, spalled concrete at an anchorage) | A tendon tells you it is unhappy in the first hour, at the anchorage, and it tells somebody standing there looking at it. After the pockets are closed nobody will see these ends again for the life of the building. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `behind-the-jack` | You stepped in behind the ram. A tendon holds its energy along its own axis, so a strand that snaps or a wedge that does not hold comes back out of the anchorage the way the jack is pointing, at a speed nothing stops. That space is the one place on this deck nobody stands in without a reason to be there, and OSHA 1926.701(c) is written that way: no employee behind the jack during tensioning except the ones essential to the operation, with signs and barriers keeping everybody else out of the area. Being curious is not being essential. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `green-concrete` | You started stressing on the calendar instead of on a strength result. Concrete that has not reached the strength the drawings require at transfer cannot carry the anchorage: the bearing plate punches through the slab face and takes the tendon and the concrete around it with it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `far-end-axis` | You stood in line with the tendon at the dead end. Both ends are on the axis. The live end is where the jack is and the end everybody remembers; the dead end is the one people walk past, and a strand that pulls through an anchorage exits there just as hard. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `hand-seat` | You went at a loaded anchorage with a hammer. The wedges are seated hydraulically by the jack, and struck by hand they can come out of the cavity at the person holding the hammer. Nothing is dressed, tapped or adjusted on an anchorage that is holding load. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Mast Climber (SmartCiti.X · `mast-climber`)

**Category:** Construction & Structural Trades  
**Trade:** Mast climber erector / ironworker  
**Certification / standard claimed:** Ironworkers / LIUNA — mast climbing work platform erector; ANSI A92.9 for MCWP design, erection and use; OSHA 29 CFR 1926.451 scaffold general requirements and 1926.502 fall protection  
**Tagline:** Adding a mast section and climbing: tie spacing, plumb, rated load and its distribution, overload cut-out proven, emergency descent rehearsed  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-21 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Take the manufacturer's erection plan | Select | A mast climber is engineered per installation. Tie spacing, free-standing height and rated load all come off the manufacturer's plan for this building, and none of them transfer from the last job. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Walk the base before anything moves | Find (any order: material under the travel path, soft ground at the base frame) | Everything above depends on the base. A mast climber transfers its whole load and its whole overturning moment into a few square feet of ground, and the ground is the part nobody inspects. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Read the load distribution chart | Select | The rated load assumes a distribution. The same total weight stacked at one end is a different machine from the same weight spread along the deck, and the chart is where that difference is written down. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Load the deck to the chart | Carry and place | Load near the mast is carried by the mast. Load at the outboard end is a lever on it. The chart's zones are the same machine rated three different ways depending on where you put things. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Close the access gate | Select | The gate is the only break in the guardrail and it is exactly where people are standing when the platform starts. Self-closing is a design feature, not a substitute for closing it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Hoist the new mast section | Carry and place | The section goes up on the erection hoist that is part of the machine, guided rather than swung. A section that arrives crooked will not engage its rack, and forcing it damages the teeth the drive climbs on. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Pin the section, all four corners | Sequence (strict order: pin 1, pin 2, pin 3, pin 4) | Four pins and four retainers, checked by eye. A section running on three is carrying the whole machine on a joint the engineer never analysed, and the missing one is always found afterwards. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Fit the wall tie at the plan's spacing | Carry and place | Ties are what turn a tall free-standing mast into a braced one. Their spacing is computed against the mast's section properties and the wind load on the platform, which is why it is a number on a drawing and not a judgement. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Torque the tie bolts | Timed hold | A loose tie lets the mast move against the building, which works the anchor and enlarges its hole until the tie is decorative. The figure is on the plan for the anchor that was installed, not the anchor you would have chosen. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Plumb the mast | Graded reading | Out of plumb, the mast carries its own weight partly as a bending load, and the drive fights the rack the whole way up. Tolerance is a few millimetres over the mast height, and it is adjusted at the base and the ties. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Prove the overload cut-out | Graded reading | The overload cut-out is the last thing between a mis-loaded deck and a buckled mast, and it is the one safety device on the machine that is never exercised in normal use. It gets proven, not assumed. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Rehearse the emergency descent | Timed hold | When the power fails the platform comes down on the manual descent, operated by whoever is on the deck. That is not the moment to read the label, so it is rehearsed while everything is working. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 13 | Take the platform up to the new working height | Tracked hold | The drive is held, not latched, so that letting go stops the machine. The rate band is where the pinion and the rack are designed to mesh — driving it hard chews the rack, and the rack is the thing holding the platform up. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 14 | Sign the platform over | Select | The tag is what tells the trades who use this platform tomorrow that somebody competent last checked it, at this height, with these ties. Without it the machine is out of service, whatever its condition. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `overload-pallet` | You loaded the block pallet onto the deck. That is more than the platform's rated capacity in one place, and a mast climber does not sag to warn you — the rack-and-pinion drive stalls or the mast buckles at the section above the last tie. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `climb-untied` | You took the platform up past the last tie. The tie spacing is what the mast is designed against; above it the mast is a free-standing cantilever carrying a moving load, and the free-standing height on the data plate is a limit, not a guideline. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `gate-open` | You left the deck's access gate open. It is the one opening in the guardrail, it is at the exact place people step on and off, and it self-closes for a reason. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `ground-clutter` | There is material stacked under the platform's travel path. A mast climber comes down onto whatever is beneath it, and the base frame does not know the difference between a kerb and a gas bottle. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

# Entertainment & Live Events

## Rigging Loft (SmartCiti.X · `rigging-loft`)

**Category:** Entertainment & Live Events  
**Trade:** Theatrical rigger (IATSE)  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IATSE — ETCP Certified Rigger, Arena  
**Tagline:** Counterweight fly-system operation: arbor inspection, balance, cued flying and automation cue programming  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-21 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the rigging plot | Select | The plot is the plan for this line set specifically. Capacity, load and trim are not the same from one batten to the next — the house was built with different rope sets, different head blocks and different grid steel over each one, and an ETCP rigger's first move is always to read which of those is above them today. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Walk the counterweight system | Find (any order: unlocked arbor on line 3, frayed lift line on line 5, unsecured counterweight brick) | A pre-show rigging check is a search across every line set on the rail, not a glance at the one you are about to fly. The set that hurts somebody tonight is almost never the one in the cue sheet — it is the one nobody was working, left unlocked or half-loaded by the crew before you, with its faults sitting in plain sight all afternoon. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Check comms with the fly floor | Select | Every cue on this rail runs over that channel, and a headset that has gone one-way sounds exactly like a deck that is not answering. Check it before the first move, because the failure you are guarding against is calling 'clear', hearing nothing, and reading the silence as agreement. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Post a floor watch | Select | A rigger at the pin rail is looking at a rope and an arbor, not at the stage. The floor watch covers the one place the operator physically cannot see — directly beneath the load — and they hold that job for the whole move, not until they get bored of it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Bring the batten in to load position | Tracked hold | Counterweight goes on at the deck, not at height, so the batten comes to a working position first. An empty batten is arbor-heavy — it wants to run up, not down — so the whole way in is the operator holding a set that is trying to get away from them, at a speed the deck can watch. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Check the batten's rated capacity | Select | The plate is the hard limit for this batten, its rope set, its head block and the grid steel above it — 220 lb here against 190 lb planned. A line set is rated as a whole system, so the weakest component in that chain is the number on the plate, and nothing about the plot overrides it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Load the arbor to balance | Graded reading | A balanced arbor is what lets one person hold two hundred pounds of scenery on a rope. Out of balance, the difference is what the operator's hands are carrying: twenty pounds out is a set that drifts, sixty pounds out is a set that runs, and at that point the rope is deciding where the batten goes instead of the rigger. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Lock the arbor rail | Select | The rope lock is what keeps a balanced arbor where you left it when you step away from the rail. It is worth knowing what it is not: it holds a set that is already in balance, and it is not rated to stop one that is running — which is why it goes on after the arbor balances, never instead of balancing it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Call the fly cue | Sequence (strict order: call clear, call moving, call stopped) | The three calls are a protocol, not a courtesy. 'Clear' is a question the deck answers; 'moving' is a commitment that the answer came back; 'stopped' releases everyone from watching. IATSE houses run this call-and-answer on every move because the alternative is an operator deciding on their own that silence meant yes. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Lock off at trim height | Select | The trim pin is a second, physical lock that holds the set exactly where the cue left it, independent of the rope lock at the rail. Two independent means of securing a load overhead is the same redundancy principle OSHA applies to anything suspended over people: one of them is allowed to fail. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Teach the automation winch's cue positions | Sequence (strict order: park position, mid-show position, full-out position) | A motorised line plays back a taught cue exactly as recorded and has no opinion about what is underneath it. Teach full-out before mid-show and the batten runs straight past the position an actor has been blocked to stand at — on stage, live, at cue speed, with the console operator's hand nowhere near it. The order you teach is the order it runs. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Save the automation cue | Select | An unsaved position list is just where the winch happened to stop, and it is gone when the console is powered down between the tech and the house opening. Saving it as a numbered cue is what lets the board operator fire it on one button in a blackout, which is the only way it will ever actually be run. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 13 | Run the cue at rehearsal speed and verify the deck | Timed hold | A powered hoist over a stage is proved at rehearsal speed with a hand on the console and a clear deck, the way the ANSI-accredited entertainment rigging standards ask any powered system to be proved before it carries a show. The first full-speed run of a new cue should never be the first time anyone finds out where it goes. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 14 | Sign the rigging log | Select | The next operator on this rail is a different person on a different call, and what they inherit is whatever the log says: load on the batten, trim height, which locks are on. A set left loaded and unrecorded is the one somebody unlocks in the dark expecting it to be empty. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `standing-under-load` | You are standing directly beneath a loaded batten and arbor. A counterweight brick dropped from a loading gallery forty feet up arrives at around thirty miles an hour and gives no warning worth the name — which is why every ETCP rigging practice and OSHA's overhead-load rules come down to the same sentence: nobody works or walks under a flown line while it can still move. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `unlocked-rail` | That arbor's locking rail pin is out and the line set is not secured. A rope lock is designed to hold a balanced set, not to arrest an unbalanced one — an arbor that starts to run with weight on it will burn straight through the lock's jaws and take whatever is in its path with it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `shortcut-call` | That is an override call button that skips the clearance check. 'Clear' is a question and 'moving' is a commitment, and calling the second before the first has come back is how a batten comes down on someone who never heard the cue. Skipping the call-and-answer is the one thing no house rigging protocol allows. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `excess-bricks` | That stack puts the arbor past the batten's rated capacity. Overloading a line set overhauls the operator at the rail, takes the rope out of its rated range, and loads the grid steel and the head block past what the ANSI-accredited entertainment rigging standards assumed when the house was built. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Stage Power (SmartCiti.X · `stage-power`)

**Category:** Entertainment & Live Events  
**Trade:** Stage / touring electrician  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IATSE — ETCP Certified Entertainment Electrician; NEC Article 520 (theaters) and 525 / single-pole separable connector (cam-lock) sequence; NFPA 70E qualified for the verification  
**Tagline:** Company switch tie-in: lockout, live-dead-live, cam-locks ground-first, cover and strain relief, energise, phase check, load test  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-21 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the power plot | Select | The plot says what the venue can give and what the rig needs, in amps, before any cam goes on. A 400 A switch feeding a 600 A rig is a decision made here at the plot, not discovered when the breaker trips at the top of the show. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Open and lock the company switch | Turn | The switch is opened and locked before the tap cover comes off. Your lock is the only thing standing between the house electrician's hand on the same switch and your own hands still on the lugs behind it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Arc-rated PPE for the verification | Select | Proving it dead is the one moment in this whole tie-in where you are working on something that might still be live. OSHA's electrical rules point straight at NFPA 70E for the PPE category; the meter goes nowhere near the lugs without that gear on first. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Live-dead-live | Sequence (strict order: known live source, switch lugs, known live source again) | A meter that reads zero can mean a dead switch or a meter with a dead battery, a blown fuse, or a broken lead. Testing it on a known live source before and after the reading is the only way that zero actually means something. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Make the cams ground-first | Sequence (strict order: ground (green), neutral (white), phase A, phase B, phase C) | Ground first so the distro frame is bonded before any conductor could be hot; neutral before phases so a load never sees a floating neutral even for an instant. Off reverses the whole order: phases first, ground last. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Strain-relieve the feeder | Select | The feeder is heavy and the whole load-in crew walks and rolls cases over it for the next several hours. The strain relief takes that pull at the frame so the cam connectors themselves never do. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Cover the taps | Select | Nothing gets energised with a bare lug sitting exposed behind an open tap cover. The cover goes back on before the switch goes on, every single time, with no exception for a quick test. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Remove the lock and energise | Turn | Your lock, your key, your call to re-energise. The area is cleared first because the first energisation after a tie-in is exactly the moment a wiring fault, if there is one, finally shows itself. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Read the phases at the distro | Graded reading | Three phases reading right at the distro, phase to neutral, is the actual proof the tie-in was made correctly. A lost or reversed neutral shows up here first — as one phase reading high and one low — long before it shows as a rack of dead dimmers onstage. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Load test | Timed hold | Balanced under an actual load is the last proof a tie-in can give. A connection that reads perfectly with nothing drawing current and then sags the moment the first cue calls for power was never actually right. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Walk the feeder run | Find (any order: damaged feeder jacket) | The feeder lives on the floor for the rest of the load-in with every road case and every foot in the building walking over it. This walk is what finds the cut in the jacket before it becomes a fault at the top of the show. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `phase-first` | You reached for a phase cam before the ground was on. Single-pole connectors are made ground first and broken ground last, so there is never a moment the distro frame is hot with no path back — that order is the whole reason cams are allowed. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `wet-distro` | The distro is standing in the puddle from the load-in. A metal frame carrying three phases on a wet floor is a shock path to whoever touches it next; it gets moved and dried before it gets power. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `taped-cam` | That cam is held in with tape because the latch is broken. A connector that can pull apart under load arcs — and a 400 A arc on a stage is a fire and a burn, not a fault. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `meter-no-gloves` | You went at the switch with the meter and no gloves or face shield. Verification is the live part of this job; it is done in the PPE the arc-flash label calls for, or it is not done. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Chain Hoist (SmartCiti.X · `chain-hoist`)

**Category:** Entertainment & Live Events  
**Trade:** Entertainment rigger — chain motors  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IATSE — ETCP Certified Rigger (Arena); ANSI E1.6-1 powered hoists; manufacturer chain-motor inspection and load-rating compliance  
**Tagline:** Flying a truss: load calc, chain inspection, bridle angle, moused hooks, test lift, deck clear, trim and lock  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-21 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the rigging plot | Select | The plot is the load, the points and the beams they hang from, with the dynamic factor already worked into the numbers. Every figure on it is checked against the venue's structure before a rigger clips onto anything and goes up. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Calculate the point load | Graded reading | The point load is the truss plus every fixture and every foot of cable on it, divided across the points, with the bridle angle multiplying whatever each leg actually carries. It is calculated and written down against ASME B30.16's own margins — not estimated by eye from the deck. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Inspect each motor's load chain | Sequence (any order: motor 1 chain, motor 2 chain) | The chain is the only thing between the beam and the truss once the motor takes weight. A twist or a stretched link is found by hand, before the motor is under load, using the same rejection criteria ASME B30.16 sets for load chain. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Set the bridle angle | Graded reading | A wide bridle multiplies the load in each leg rather than sharing it evenly. At a 120-degree included angle, each leg is already carrying the full point load on its own; the plot's angle limit is the one the sling legs were actually rated for. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Mouse the hooks | Sequence (any order: motor 1 hook, motor 2 hook) | Mousing keeps the sling captured in the hook's throat through a bounce or a swing during the show, when nobody is standing under the point to watch it. It costs thirty seconds to close; a hook that walks off under load costs the whole truss. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Test lift | Timed hold | The test lift proves the points, the slings and the chain under the real, calculated load while the truss is still a few inches off the deck, where a failure drops a few inches onto an empty floor instead of a rigger's head. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Clear the deck | Select | The go is called only by the person who has personally looked under the whole length of the truss and heard clear back from the deck crew — never assumed from across the room, and never called from memory of the last cue. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Fly the truss to trim | Tracked hold | Motors that run at even slightly different speeds tilt the truss and shift its load onto whichever point is still carrying the higher end. The rigger watches the level indicator the whole climb and stops the faster motor the moment it drifts. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Mark the trim and lock the motors | Select | A locked controller cannot be bumped by a stray foot or a curious hand once the show is running under that truss. The trim mark is the reference every later call — bump-in, cue, strike — gets checked against for the rest of the run. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Walk the points before the house opens | Find (any order: shackle pin not seated) | Hardware is checked again by eye and by hand after the fly itself, because the act of lifting is exactly what moves a hardware connection that looked fine sitting still on the deck. This walk is the last look before a house full of people sits underneath it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `under-truss` | You called the lift with a crew member under the truss. Nothing goes up over a body; the deck is cleared and confirmed before the go, every lift. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `kinked-chain` | You ran a motor with a kinked, twisted chain. A twist in the load chain jams in the hoist under load and the motor drops the point — that chain is tagged out, not run. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `hook-not-moused` | You lifted on a hook with no mousing. A hook without its latch or mousing can walk off the sling under a bounce; the point that unhooks is the point the truss swings on. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `over-rating` | You added the point-load to a motor already at its rated capacity. A one-tonne motor is a one-tonne motor; the fix is another point, not a hope. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Fly System (SmartCiti.X · `fly-system`)

**Category:** Entertainment & Live Events  
**Trade:** Theatrical rigger / fly operator  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IATSE — ETCP Certified Rigger (Theatre); ANSI E1.4-1 manual counterweight rigging systems; OSHA 29 CFR 1910.28 fall protection on the loading bridge; venue 'heads up' and lineset-tagging procedure  
**Tagline:** Counterweight lineset load: rail locked, deck cleared with a call, batten in, fixtures hung and safetied, loader clipped in on the bridge, bricks to match the pipe with spreaders and a lock ring, balanced test lift, trim, lock, clamp, tag  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-21 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the hang schedule | Select | The schedule says what goes on the pipe and what that weighs; the arbor is loaded to that number, not to how the rope feels. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Lock the rail | Select | Nothing is hung on a pipe that can move. The lock holds the lineset while it goes out of balance during loading; the operator is the second lock. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Call and clear the deck | Sequence (any order: 'heads up' call, deck clear under the batten) | The call is the only warning a stage has. Nobody stands under a pipe that is about to move, and the fly operator does not move it until the deck answers. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Bring the batten in | Tracked hold | Steady hand-over-hand keeps the lineset under control and the arbor from bouncing in its guides. A fast fly is a fly that gets away. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Hang and safety the fixtures | Sequence (strict order: fixtures clamped, safety cables, cable pick tied off) | A clamp holds a fixture until it does not; the safety cable is what holds it then. Cable ties come last so nothing is left hanging when the pipe flies. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Clip in on the loading bridge | Sequence (any order: harness clipped to the bridge line, bridge gate closed) | The loading bridge is a catwalk over the arbor pit forty feet up with an open side to load through. The tie-off is what keeps a loader who leans out on the bridge. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Load the arbor to the pipe | Graded reading | The arbor balances the pipe plus what is on it. Light, the pipe runs to the deck; heavy, the arbor runs to the loft. The number on the schedule is the number in the arbor. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Spreader plates and lock ring | Sequence (strict order: spreader plates, lock ring) | Spreaders keep the arbor rods from bowing under a stack; the ring keeps the stack from jumping the rods if the arbor stops hard. No ring, and the bricks leave the arbor on a runaway. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Test lift | Tracked hold | The test lift is the only proof of balance. A hand on the line feels a heavy pipe or a heavy arbor before it becomes a runaway; a foot of travel is enough to know. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Fly to trim | Graded reading | Trim is where the designer wants the light. It is set once, marked on the purchase line, and the clamp holds it for the run. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Lock, clamp, tag | Sequence (strict order: rope lock, trim clamp, lineset tag) | The tag tells the next crew what is on the pipe and in the arbor; the clamp holds trim; the lock holds everything. Nobody leaves an untagged lineset. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Walk the arbor before you leave the bridge | Find (any order: brick outside the rods) | The arbor is checked from the bridge before the loader unclips. A brick outside the rods is found now or on the deck. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `unlock-unbalanced` | You released the rope lock with the batten loaded and the arbor light. A pipe-heavy lineset runs away to the deck, the arbor flies to the loft, and the purchase line takes the hands off whoever is holding it. The lock stays on until the arbor matches the pipe. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `bridge-unclipped` | You stepped onto the loading bridge without clipping in. It is a forty-foot drop to the deck through a gap the width of an arbor, and the bricks you are handling weigh more than the harness. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `under-batten` | You walked under a batten that was moving. Everything on a moving pipe — a fixture, a safety cable, a wrench — lands on the deck under it; the stage is called clear and stays clear until the pipe stops. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `toss-brick` | You tossed a brick to the bridge instead of handing it. A counterweight brick is twenty pounds of steel; dropped from the bridge it goes through the deck, and through whoever is on it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Broadcast Truck (SmartCiti.X · `broadcast-truck`)

**Category:** Entertainment & Live Events  
**Trade:** Outside-broadcast / utility electrician  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IATSE broadcast and IBEW utility electricians; NEC Article 525 (carnivals, fairs and similar events) and Article 530 (motion picture and television studios); NFPA 70E qualified for the lockout/verification; OSHA 29 CFR 1910.147 lockout/tagout on the generator breaker; NEC 250.6 and 702 on generator neutral-ground bonding and objectionable current  
**Tagline:** Generator power to an outside-broadcast truck: neutral-ground bond checked, camloks landed ground-first, GFCI proven, and the transfer to shore power held clean without dropping the truck's UPS  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-21 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the event power plan | Select | The plan says whether one generator or two carries this show, and which NEC article governs the tie-in — a fairground stage under Article 525 is landed differently than a permanent studio under Article 530, and that distinction is settled here, before any cable is uncoiled. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Open and lock the generator's output breaker | Turn | The breaker is opened and locked before a single camlok touches it. Your lock is the only thing that stops somebody else closing it while your hands are on the lugs — the same rule that governs every panel on this circuit, generator or building. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Check the generator's neutral-ground bond | Graded reading | A portable generator standing as the only source on site has to have its neutral bonded to ground right there, at the generator — that bond is what makes a ground fault downstream trip a breaker instead of just sitting there energised. An open reading here means nothing downstream will ever trip on a fault until somebody finds that out the hard way. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Carry the feeder whip to the truck | Carry and place | This whip is the only conductor between a generator somebody else sized and a truck full of gear that cannot brown out mid-broadcast. It is carried and seated before anything on either end is energised, the same as any other feeder run on this job. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Land the camlok connectors ground-first | Sequence (strict order: ground (green), neutral (white), phase A, phase B, phase C) | Ground first so the frame and the whole feeder are bonded before any conductor could be hot; neutral before phases so nothing downstream ever sees a floating neutral, even for a second. Breaking the tie-in reverses the order — phases off first, ground last — for exactly the same reason. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Strain-relieve and cover the taps | Select | The feeder gets walked on and rolled over all day at a load-in like this one. The strain relief takes that pull instead of the camlok collars, and the cover means nobody finds a live lug with a boot or a hand. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Remove the lock and energise | Turn | Your lock, your key, your call to re-energise. The area is clear because the first breaker close is the moment a fault in the feeder or the panel shows itself. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Read the phases at the truck's distro | Graded reading | Three phases reading right at the truck's own distro is the proof the tie-in and the cam order both came out correct. A lost neutral shows here first — one leg high, one low — long before it shows as a rack of gear browning out on air. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Test the field cable's GFCI | Timed hold | A GFCI that has never been tested is a GFCI nobody has actually proven will open on a ground fault. It gets pressed and confirmed before the cable run goes out across the field, where an audience is standing on the other end of it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Ease the transfer to shore power | Tracked hold | The truck's UPS only bridges a gap it doesn't have to fill — dropped for even a moment, everything downstream that isn't already on battery blinks, and a video wall or router that blinks mid-broadcast doesn't just flicker, it drops sync and takes minutes to recover. Held too long the other way, generator and shore power are briefly paralleled on the same bus, which neither source's protection is built to survive. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Confirm the transfer switch is switched-neutral | Select | With the neutral switched, only one source is ever bonded to ground at a time. A switch that leaves the neutral solid ties the generator's bond and the shore power's bond together every moment both sources are connected — exactly the parallel path NEC 250.6 exists to prevent. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Walk the field cable run | Find (any order: damaged cable jacket, missing ground pin, cable across the walkway with no ramp) | The tie-in is the interesting part and the field run is where it actually gets tested — foot traffic, weather and the show's own gear all live on this cable, and none of these three faults show up until somebody is standing on one. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 13 | Load test on generator power | Timed hold | A tie-in that reads right unloaded and sags the moment the truck's full load comes up was never actually proven. The load test is what separates a connection that looks correct from one that holds through an actual broadcast. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 14 | Close out before the show goes live | Find (any order: ground rod bonded to the chassis, GFCI test tag, spill containment under the generator) | None of these are the interesting part of the job, and all three are what an incident report looks for first: the ground rod that's actually bonded, the tag that proves the GFCI was tested today, and the containment that keeps a leak off the ground instead of in it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `phase-first` | You reached for a phase camlok before the ground was landed at the generator's own distro panel. Landing ground first is what keeps the frame bonded before any conductor downstream could be hot — skip it, and a fault anywhere on this feeder has no path back to the source until somebody notices the hard way. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `wet-camlok` | The generator's distro is standing in the runoff pooling by the ice truck. A metal frame carrying three phases on wet ground is a shock path to whoever touches it next, and it gets moved to dry, level ground before it carries any load at all. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `double-bond` | You landed a second bonding jumper between neutral and ground at the truck, on top of the one already at the generator. With the transfer switch already switching the neutral, that second bond opens a parallel path for return current over the safety-ground conductor and the truck's own chassis — the exact 'objectionable current' NEC 250.6 exists to keep off equipment enclosures, and it turns every metal case on this truck into part of the path. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `gfci-bypass` | You ran the camera position's power off a plain stinger cable instead of the GFCI-protected field run. That cable is going out across ground the audience walks on, in whatever weather the show gets, and the GFCI on the proper run is the only thing standing between a nicked jacket out there and somebody getting the fault current instead of a tripped breaker. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Arena Rigging (SmartCiti.X · `arena-rigging`)

**Category:** Entertainment & Live Events  
**Trade:** Arena rigger — up-rigger / down-rigger  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IATSE stage locals — ETCP Certified Rigger (Arena); the ESTA E1 series for entertainment rigging practice; OSHA 29 CFR 1926 Subpart M fall protection; the venue's rigging grid drawing and the production's rigging plot as the governing documents  
**Tagline:** Setting a point in the roof steel: the point called and confirmed both ways, the bridle worked out on the plot, the beam checked against the grid drawing, the floor cleared and watched, tied off to structure the whole time  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-21 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Take the production's rigging plot | Select | Every point on a load-in exists on one document, drawn by the production's rigging designer. The point number, the bay it lands in, the load it carries and whether it is a dead-hang or a bridle are all decided there, before anybody leaves the floor. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Read the venue's rigging grid drawing | Select | The plot says what the show wants. The grid drawing says what this building will give it — per point and per beam, from the engineer who signed the drawing. When the two disagree, the building wins, and the point moves. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Dress for the steel | Sequence (any order: harness and twin-leg lanyard, tools tethered, headset to the floor) | Three things go up with you and all three are somebody else's safety as much as yours: a harness with two legs so you are never unattached, tethers so nothing you carry can arrive on the floor, and a headset so the person who cannot see you can still talk to you. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Tie off to structure before you go out | Select | Fall protection in the steel is continuous, not occasional, and the anchorage is building structure. A rigger moving along a beam is attached to something at all times — that is the rule the whole shift is built on, and there is no part of the job that is worth the exception. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Take the point, find it, confirm it both ways | Sequence (strict order: take the call off the floor, count the bays to the tag, plumb it and get it confirmed) | Up-rigger and down-rigger are one job done by two people who cannot see each other. The point is called from the floor, found in the steel, and confirmed in both directions before anything is hung — because a point in the wrong bay is a point that loads a beam nobody checked. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Clear the floor under the point and keep it clear | Sequence (strict order: tape the drop zone, spotter on the zone, clear confirmed up) | Tape alone is a suggestion; a load-in floor walks through tape all day. The zone is taped, then given to a person who stands there and keeps it empty, and only then is the clear called up. Cleared and kept clear are two different jobs and the second one is the one that lasts. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Work out the tension in each bridle leg | Graded reading | A bridle is arithmetic, not a feeling. Each leg carries its share of the vertical load divided by the cosine of the leg's angle from vertical, so two legs never carry half each — they carry more, and the flatter you spread them the more it becomes. This is the number the sling, the shackle and the beam are all chosen against. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Check what the bridle does to each beam | Graded reading | A bridle leg does not just hang off a beam, it drags it towards the other one. The flatter the legs, the larger that horizontal component gets, and a beam rated to carry weight downwards is not automatically rated to be pulled sideways. The grid drawing gives both figures and both have to be satisfied. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Take the steel out to the beam | Carry and place | The steel goes out once, to the beam the plot named, on a rigger who is attached the whole way. Every extra trip along a beam is another transition, and transitions are where riggers come off. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Basket the beam and seat the shackle | Turn | A basket round the beam puts the load into the section rather than into an edge, and the shackle pin is either all the way home or it is not in. A pin two turns out looks exactly like a pin that is in, and it walks out over a show's worth of movement. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Lower the drop line on the call | Tracked hold | The drop line is the first thing that goes from the steel to the floor and it sets the habit for everything after it. It goes on a call, into a zone somebody is watching, at a rate a person on the ground can follow with their eyes. A rope run out fast is indistinguishable from a rope dropped. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Haul the point up to the steel | Timed hold | What comes up is heavier than what went down and it swings. A steady haul keeps it under the point instead of pendulum-ing across the bay into the other crew's steel, and the hold is what stops it being dropped back through the zone halfway up. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 13 | Tag the point and confirm it to the floor | Select | The tag is how the next person — the motor crew, the head rigger, the venue at strike — knows which point this is without going back to the plot. The read-back closes the loop the call opened: the floor hears its own point number come back from the steel. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 14 | Walk the beam before you come out of the steel | Find (any order: sling bearing on a bare edge, loose shackle left in the steel) | The last thing an up-rigger does is leave the bay in a state they would be happy to stand underneath. Everything that gets caught up there gets caught by a rigger looking, not by an inspection later — by then the floor has a show on it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `hung-anchor` | You clipped your lanyard to the point you are hanging. An anchorage is structure — a beam, a purlin, a column — and nothing else. A sling, a shackle, a motor chain or a point somebody else hung is a load path that is there because somebody put it there this morning, and it can come out the same way. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `free-transition` | You unhooked both legs to get past the purlin. Continuous attachment means one leg is always on something before the other comes off; the transition is the only moment in the whole shift where a rigger is genuinely free, and it is the moment they fall in. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `loose-tool` | You took an untethered wrench out onto the beam. Anything carried into the steel is tethered or it is not carried, because the floor below fills with crew, forklifts and eventually an audience, and a dropped shackle from roof height arrives with no warning at all. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `last-show-mark` | You hung off the paint mark the last show left on the steel. That mark records where somebody else's point went, with somebody else's load, possibly before the venue's drawing was revised. What this beam will take comes off the venue's rigging grid drawing, and nothing else. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `open-floor` | You called for the drop line over a floor that was still open. Nothing comes down — not a rope, not a tape, not a shackle — until the area under the point is taped off and somebody on the ground is watching it and has said so. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
